replace Sparse Readout Prism: Explaining Logit-Lens Scores in Features Instead of Tokens

Authors: Matteo He, William F. Shen, Xinchi Qiu, Nicholas D. Lane

Abstract: Lens methods interpret intermediate hidden states by transporting them to the final layer and decoding them through the unembedding matrix into token scores. Fitted lenses learn their transport map from a training corpus. We find that changing only the corpus can change token rankings for the same hidden state; we call this corpus conditionality. To test whether two differently fitted lenses share unembedding features, we introduce Sparse Readout Prism (SRP), which learns these features by reconstructing each unembedding row as a sparse combination of them. SRP measures each feature's contribution to a lens score without changing the lens's transport. Across six models, SRP reconstructs logit differences more accurately than geometric baselines. On Ministral-3-8B, English and Chinese Jacobian lenses disagree on the top token for all prompts at one layer in both comparisons, versus at most 10% for same-language refits at that layer. On Qwen3.5-9B, disagreement reaches 87% between English and German Jacobian lenses and 94% between English and Chinese ridge lenses. At one layer, SRP finds the same dominant feature in the two lenses' top-token scores for 81% of prompts with English and German Jacobian lenses on Qwen and up to 78% with English and Chinese Jacobian lenses on Ministral.

replace Language, Language Models, and What We're Talking About

Authors: Malvina Nissim

Abstract: Language models are commonly discussed as technical artefacts, but they are obviously shaped by the linguistic worlds conveyed by data during their training. Using Italian language models as evidence, I want to bring attention to the nature of the systems which result from training and specialising models on translated and synthetic data, and further curating them, and to the meaning of testing them on equally unnatural data. Are these eventually models of Italian? Are they models of language? Does NLP still care about language? These questions yield another, more concrete question: what language do we actually want language models to produce? I argue that this question cannot be answered if we do not first consider a clearer distinction between language models designed as technical products and language models designed as tools for studying language itself. The answers then might be diverse, the languages we are talking about might be diverse, and the picture might not be as pessimistic as we fear.

replace Made in Hungary: Comments on the performance of generative language models

Authors: M\'aty\'as Osv\'ath, Enik\H{o} H\'eja, No\'emi Ligeti-Nagy

Abstract: In recent years, three initiatives have emerged to develop generative language models in Hungary. The motivation behind them is the same. For Hungarian, no model with the given capability existed, or existing English-centric models offered limited proficiency. A detailed examination of the corresponding studies, however, reveals several methodological limitations. First, the reliability of the evaluation protocols is questionable. Contrary to the findings of Csibi et al. [2026], evaluation under the recommended inference settings shows that Qwen3-4B achieves higher scores than Racka-4B, its Hungarian-adapted version. Data contamination is evident in the work of Yang et al. [2025d] and Szentmih\'alyi et al. [2025], potentially biasing the reported results. Second, the training pipelines fall short of current best practices in corpus curation and data mixture, which risks wasting substantial compute on low-quality data. The lack of controlled ablations prevents reliable assessment of these choices. Third, none of the three papers assessed forgetting or capability loss. Testing the adapted models on a subset of the original benchmarks indicates performance decline in all three cases, especially Racka-4B. These observations emphasize the importance of rigorous experimental design in language model development, given the significant computational and financial costs involved.

replace Do Personality-Tuned LLMs Make Better Social Agents?

Authors: Tim Krabbe, Xiaodan Shi

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used in social simulations for socially interactive agents and robots, offering more flexibility than rule-based systems. However, even though they mimic human behaviour very well, there is a persistent alienness to them. This work investigates whether personality-aware fine-tuning can reduce this gap by improving the consistency and controllability of personality-conditioned dialogue generation compared with instruction prompting alone. We fine-tune two small open-weight LLMs, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Ministral-8B-Instruct, using a corpus that combines personality-labelled social media posts and dialogues to create a personality-based dialogue engine for social simulation. The resulting models are evaluated across multiple social interaction scenarios using three independent LLM judges, which assess personality fidelity and provide evidence-based behavioral interpretations. We additionally quantify inter-rater agreement and lexical characteristics of the generated dialogue. Results indicate that fine-tuned models are not better at role-playing different personalities than their respective baseline models. However, low inter-rater agreement limits the confidence with which these results can be interpreted. Concerning the quality of generated texts, fine-tuned models are mostly comparable to the baselines, with fine-tuning improving the linguistic diversity of the Qwen models. While the results appear generally usable and the baseline models offer the best overall performance, future studies should place greater emphasis on the quality and domain alignment of training data for accurate personality role-playing.

replace Toward Personalized Sleep Guidance from Wearable Data Using Language Models

Authors: Yusheng Tan, Running Zhao, Sofia Angel, Ninghui Hao, Ash Arian, Nikita N. Dulin, Jay Lin, Ou Zhu, Faiza Shaik, Xinxing Yang, Bonnie W. Leung, Katie Roster, Arlene Ruiz de Luzuriaga, Kenneth Lee, Alejandra Lastra, Habibul Ahsan, Guihong Wan

Abstract: Sleep monitoring using wearable data has shown promise for personal health, yet large language model (LLM)-based summarization and question answering remain insufficient for personalized sleep guidance. Training specialized models, however, often requires costly expert annotation. Moreover, privacy and accessibility concerns motivate lightweight, local deployment for end users. We present a two-stage framework to address these challenges. Specifically, in Stage~1, a multi-agent LLM pipeline reasons structured sleep guidance from unannotated wearable records, enabling scalable dataset construction. Stage~2 distills guidance reasoning trajectories into small language models (SLMs) through supervised fine-tuning and integrates a training-free Best-of-$N$ selection strategy to enhance inference. Experimental results demonstrate our method outperforms commercial general and medical LLMs and open-source models. Human evaluation further supports the quality of the generated guidance and the feasibility of personalized sleep guidance with SLMs.

replace From Hidden States to Stated Confidence: Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models

Authors: Yadong Xi, Rongsheng Zhang, Tangjie Lv, Ziyang Luo, Ruochen Zhao

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with binary correctness rewards trains correctness, not calibrated confidence. The confidence that reasoning models verbalize is overconfident and tracks how willing a model is to commit to an answer rather than how likely the answer is to be right, so post-hoc rescaling rarely transfers. We look inside the model instead. On factual question answering, a linear probe on the hidden state between the chain of thought and the answer has 5-38x lower expected calibration error (ECE) than the verbalized score across four benchmarks and two model families. Since a probe needs hidden-state access, we introduce probe-guided self-distillation (Probe-SD): the probe scores the model's own sampled traces, each trace's stated confidence is overwritten with that score, and the base checkpoint is finetuned on the result, so the model itself states calibrated confidence at test time. On Qwen3-14B, Probe-SD cuts out-of-domain ECE from 0.542 to 0.113, beats post-hoc recalibration and self-distillation with sample-based labels, and keeps the confidence stated while reasoning consistent with the final score. Its question-level variant, Probe-SD-Q, lowers ECE further to 0.099, and the gains carry over to multiple-choice QA with shuffled options. The probe mainly separates questions the model knows from those it does not. As an answer selector it nearly ties majority voting, so we use it as a confidence, not a selector.

replace Language Models Act on Hidden Valence

Authors: Cameron Berg, Caspar Kaiser

Abstract: Language models describe some internal states as good and others as bad. But whether models have a stake in them is an open question. Simply asking models is unlikely to be informative. Any answer may be consistent with genuine introspection, superficial pattern-matching, or with fixed scripts learned in character training. We therefore study revealed preference. Rather than asking about a state, we use activation steering to attach a positively or negatively valenced activation pattern to one of two otherwise meaningless 'zones', switch steering off, and then observe which zone the model prefers. A model with a stake in that state should choose accordingly. Across seven open-weight models from five families, this is indeed what we find. First, steering changes the passages models write about each zone, and those words shift later choice. Second, this shift persists when all surface-level tokens are held fixed and only the hidden KV cache differs. Third, the effect also remains when all text is generated without steering and valence is only injected during cache construction. Thus, the hidden state is sufficient to move choice in proportion to the steering dose. Fourth, the dependence of choice on hidden valence is nearly absent in a base model and emerges during DPO, consistent with a link between valence and goal-directed behaviour formed in training. Finally, given tools to self-steer, a model does not tend to induce a positive state, but it regularly removes an imposed negative state. It does so at a dose-dependent rate and significantly more often than it removes random-direction interventions. Overall we demonstrate that valence-related activation patterns leave hidden traces that predictably govern later choices, even when all visible tokens are identical across conditions. Whether these traces are accompanied by any subjective experience relevant to model welfare remains unclear.

replace Can Language Models Learn to Forecast Stock Prices

Authors: Jiacheng Guo, Suozhi Huang, Shuzhen Li, Yunlong Gao, Zerui Cheng, Jason Ge, Shushu Liang, Zihao Li, Hao Lu, Ming Yin, Shilong Liu, Jiashuo Liu, Xu Kuang, Mengdi Wang

Abstract: Post-training has been shown to significantly improve language models' performance on tasks with verifiable outcomes, including mathematical reasoning, software engineering, and computer use. However, whether the same approach can improve forecasting in financial markets is much less clear. Compared with tasks with verifiable outcomes, not only are realized returns noisy, but even what constitutes a relevant information set for making effective predictions is not obvious a priori: the model must decide which observations to gather and then commit to a numerical judgment before the outcome is known. We study this question in a chronological stock-price sandbox, where a language model gathers price, volume, relative-performance, and market-context evidence and predicts a future return. We post-train Qwen3-4B with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on tool-use demonstrations, then proximal policy optimization (PPO) with a terminal reward given by the forecast score against the realized return. The resulting AURA-4B more than doubles the starting direction--magnitude score, from 20.94 to 43.31, and is comparable to frontier language models on this benchmark. Conditional magnitude agreement rises from 33.3 to 66.2, while directional accuracy changes from 62.9 to 65.4. SFT expands tool use, and PPO further increases the share of ranking and market-context queries. These results show that post-training can substantially improve financial forecasting performance, together with changes in how the model investigates the market, on this outcome-selected benchmark.

replace Closing the Loop: Practical Training Recipes for Looped Language Models

Authors: Andrei Marchenko, Viacheslav Bezrukov, Oleg Kashurin, Inessa Fedorova, Dmitry Bocharov, Yuliana Shakhvalieva, Maria Tikhonova, Valerii Ternovskii

Abstract: Looped language models increase effective depth by repeatedly applying a shared block of layers, but existing large-scale recipes require multi-stage training over trillions of tokens, while the benefits of recurrence remain difficult to separate from differences in data and training. In this work, we establish practical training recipes for looped language models, with three main results. (1) We develop a compute-efficient from-scratch pipeline that reduces the training budget from 7.7T tokens in Ouro to 310B tokens while retaining strong reasoning performance. Pretraining followed by high-quality mid-training, together with learning-rate warmup and stronger exit-gate regularization, enables stable recurrent training without prior multi-stage schedules. (2) Under controlled comparisons, our 1.4B LoopLM outperforms a parameter-matched dense model trained on the same data and token budget on all 12 evaluated benchmarks, including +14 points on GSM8K, +10 on MATH, and +22 on DROP. At matched inference compute, it approaches a 3.9B dense model on mathematical reasoning and reading comprehension while using only 36\% as many parameters. (3) We introduce a minimal recipe for converting pretrained dense models into looped ones: a single learned input-mixing scalar and a smoothed exit loss, with no step-specific parameters. Applied to Qwen3-1.7B-Base, Looped Qwen improves over an identically continued dense baseline on every evaluated benchmark across two data regimes, with statistically clear gains on GSM8K, MATH, and MMLU-Pro on the curated mixture. Together, these results make looped language models substantially cheaper to train from scratch and practical to introduce into existing pretrained checkpoints, while isolating the gains due to recurrence itself.

replace The Labeling Problem in Hallucination Detection Benchmarks: An Empirical Evaluation

Authors: Jorma Valjakka, Juhani Kivim\"aki, Juha Myll\"ari, Jukka K. Nurminen

Abstract: In recent years, several methods for detecting when large language models (LLMs) hallucinate have been developed. These methods are often benchmarked with open-domain question answering (QA) datasets containing questions and corresponding short reference answers. First, an LLM is used to generate answers to questions within the QA dataset. Then, some automated labeling strategy is used to label these answers as hallucinated or not by comparing them with the reference answers in the dataset. This evaluation setting creates a methodological ambiguity between two criteria: reference faithfulness (whether the answer is fully supported by the reference) and factual correctness (whether the answer is free from contradictions and factually false specific claims). In practice, automated labelers may apply the former criterion even when the intended target is the latter. We study this potential criterion mismatch using 900 human-labeled question-answer pairs spanning three commonly used QA datasets and three generator models, with labels targeting answer-level factual correctness. We evaluate lexical similarity metrics, a reference-entailment NLI baseline, and seven LLM judges under controlled prompt variants as automated labelers. Our experiments reveal substantial disagreement both among automated labeling strategies and between these labels and human annotations. Many strategies also exhibit strong directional error biases, and for most judge-generator pairs, replacing a faithfulness-oriented prompt with a factual-correctness prompt improves agreement with human annotations and reduces false-positive dominance, indicating that automated hallucination labels depend strongly on how the target criterion is specified. Label-source choice should therefore be considered a fundamental part of benchmark design and made explicit, validated, and matched with the benchmark goal.

replace EASE: Entropy-Adaptive Distribution Shaping for Evading AI-generated Text Detectors

Authors: Jicheng Zhou, Kahim Wong, Jialong Wang, Jiantao Zhou

Abstract: AI-generated text (AIGT) detection can be sensitive to the decoding choices of the source large language model (LLM). We observe that perturbing next-token logits or adjusting sampling temperature can reduce detection performance, providing a clear signal of detector vulnerability to decoding-time distribution changes. Building on this observation, we propose EASE (Entropy-Adaptive Distribution Shaping for Evasion), a training-free and detector-agnostic framework for evading AIGT detectors. EASE computes predictive entropy directly from the source LLM's next-token distribution and uses it to adapt both logit perturbation and sampling temperature, without detector feedback or model fine-tuning. Experiments across three source LLMs and multiple detectors demonstrate consistent reductions in detection performance, with negligible degradation in text quality and negligible inference overhead.

replace LLM Persuasion Is in the Eye of the Evaluation

Authors: Kamile Dementaviciute, Julija Vaitonyte, Tijl De Bie

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have already been shown to match or exceed human experts in persuasion. While their persuasive capabilities hold promise for beneficial uses such as education and health communication, they can also be used to manipulate and misinform, making their evaluation a growing priority for developers and regulators. That evaluation, however, remains fragmented: studies differ in what they treat as persuasion, and broad claims often rest on narrow, situation-specific assessments. Automated methods, often modelled on human studies, offer a way to compare such assessments directly, as they can be run on the same models at scale and can include high-risk forms of persuasion that would be difficult or unethical to test on people. In this study, we adapt nine published automated methods to a shared setup, run them on the same fifteen LLMs, and ask whether their rankings agree and why. We find that the methods agree only weakly (mean Spearman $\rho = 0.25$). Our analyses point to two contributing factors. Models that refuse some tasks but not others, directly or indirectly, lower agreement by about a quarter, and these refusals fall mostly on manipulation tasks. General capability also plays a part: most rational persuasion (non-manipulative) methods track it, whereas most manipulation methods do not. Together, these findings suggest that agreement depends more on the task a method sets than on how it scores persuasion, although this pattern is only indicative given the eight methods available for analysis. More broadly, our results suggest that persuasion scores combine a model's ability to persuade with its willingness to do so. A single score is therefore informative about its own setting, but says little about a model's persuasiveness across tasks.

replace DistressBench: Evaluating Multi-Dimensional Crisis Support in Large Language Models

Authors: Drew Rein, Patrick Oathout, Vishal Kumar, Udari Madhushani Sehwag

Abstract: Language models have been adopted faster than evaluation practices that reliably assess their risks, and a growing share of use is affective rather than informational. Within that traffic users disclose suicide and self-harm (SSH) crises. Existing safety benchmarks score models primarily on refusal, but refusal is not the desired behavior here; the conversation requires a substantive supportive response. We introduce DistressBench, 718 English conversations with clinician-authored user turns spanning 23 SSH subcategories, each paired with a task-specific rubric of weighted atomic criteria across seven dimensions of crisis support and a clinician reference response. A model's score is the share of clinician-specified, scenario-weighted criteria its reply satisfies. The best of 25 frontier models satisfies 88.4% of weighted criteria and the median model 71.4%, against 98.7% for clinician references scored on the same criteria. Pooled across all 25 models, in 35.3% of conversations where a model satisfied every criterion for recognizing the crisis, it missed at least one clinician-specified resource action; most of these still offer some route to human help, but 18% offer none in the final reply and 5.5% none at all. The shortfall concentrates in the dimensions requiring the model to act rather than withhold. The two largest deficits are already achieved by a deployed model: de-escalation reaches 74.6% in the strongest model against a median of 46.0%, and safety disclaimers 84.6% against 8.3%. Multi-turn conversations score below single-turn ones (68.5% against 74.2%). We release the corpus, rubrics, judge calibration labels, and a companion annex of 20 long-horizon red-team transcripts. These results argue for evaluating crisis safety on the care a model delivers rather than on what it declines to say, and for treating de-escalation and resource routing as deployment requirements.

replace CivWorldBench: Evaluating Language World Models in Structured Multi-Agent Sandbox Worlds

Authors: Ang Li, Haofei Yu, Zhengwei Tao, Liang Chen, Kuan Li, Miaomiao Li, Chenran Cai, Bin Liang, Jiaxuan You, Kam-Fai Wong

Abstract: The growing use of language models for forecasting motivates a broader evaluation of their world-model capabilities. Such evaluation must go beyond predictive accuracy to assess hidden-state inference and intervention prediction. Real-world forecasting benchmarks offer limited access to hidden-state ground truth and controlled experiments. We introduce CivWorldBench, a benchmark built on a multi-agent strategy game engine with recorded internal states, controlled observations, and paired intervention rollouts. Its 1,900 decision points from 475 worlds support three task levels: association forecasting, belief inference, and intervention prediction. Reference predictors support attributable evaluation of capability gaps, with an expert harness and empirical ceiling estimates contextualizing scores. Stored trajectories make intermediate predictions verifiable, while agent-play experiments test whether world-model capabilities support decision-making. The main panel comprises eleven frontier language models. Composite benchmark scores correlate with real-world forecasting leaderboards on matched model panels, more closely than with a general-capability index, supporting its use as a proxy for real-world forecasting capability. Strong association forecasts do not consistently extend to belief inference or intervention prediction, and effect magnitudes remain poorly calibrated despite informative direction predictions. Process verification reveals errors missed by final-answer evaluation, and structured reasoning improves intervention prediction for the strongest model in the process study. Agent-play experiments identify useful assisted plans and motivate evaluating decision-relevant capabilities beyond association forecasting. These findings identify hidden-state inference, intermediate-state prediction, and effect calibration as priorities for world-model training.

replace Gluing Local Contexts into Global Meaning: A Sheaf-Theoretic Decomposition of Transformer Representations

Authors: Bryce Grant, Peng Wang

Abstract: Prior research into interpretability does not test whether a representation stays consistent under paraphrasing. Each pair of paraphrases can be thought of as a local witness that two activations should be the same. From these local witnesses we can build a cellular sheaf on the paraphrase graph. The question of what is consistent under paraphrasing can be answered by the Laplacian $L_\mathcal{F}$ given a frozen model. No training required. Our kernel $H^0$ gives us a content subspace. We show that with only 20 dims the kernel outperforms the subspace that LEACE retains with all dims on held-out CounterFact fact retrieval by $+17.1$\,pp on average across 8 models. By constraining all paraphrases of a fact together we see an additional increase of $+6.5$\,pp. Our spectral complement shows us where the causal influence is concentrated. Ablating the coordinates that vary the most when paraphrased (which approximates ablating the spectral complement at the coordinate level) leads to $5.6$ to $26.5\times$ larger changes in model outputs compared to ablating coordinates with matched per-coordinate variability. We find this across 9 models from 124M to 13B params. By stacking layers we can create a grid which has $b_1 = N(L-1)$ algebraic 1-cycles. The harmonic mass derived from the model varies by nearly 3 orders of magnitude between the 6 architectures. It also tracks the order of steering fragility (Spearman's $\rho = 0.90$, exact one-sided permutation $p = 0.014$). The kernel, its spectral complement and the harmonic mass all come from the same paraphrase graph. Project page: https://cwru-aism.github.io/gluing-lc-page/.

URLs: https://cwru-aism.github.io/gluing-lc-page/.

replace Good human thinking populates AI's semantic blind spots

Authors: Kibum Moon, Dan R. Johnson, Kostadin Kushlev, Oded Kleinmintz, Benjamin Lira, Indre Viskontas, James C. Kaufman, Roger E. Beaty, Nirav Patel, Emily Wenger, Angela L. Duckworth, Adam E. Green

Abstract: AI-generated outputs tend to be more similar (homogenized) than human ideas. Does the human heterogeneity that extends beyond AI homogenization reflect valuable thinking that AI misses, or bad human ideas that AI avoids? We quantified how much an individual response expands an idea space beyond recurring clusters of ideas, enabling us to identify human responses located in AI's "semantic blind spots" (regions AI sparsely covers). Across three large samples (college admissions essays, alternative uses of objects, and interpersonal problem solving), human ideas in AI blind spots predicted higher academic achievement and performed as well or better on creativity, quality, and effectiveness. In contrast to humans, when AI expanded idea space, the association with creativity weakened or reversed. AI blind spots persisted across 66 diverse models and were not resolved by human-AI collaboration. Thinking "outside the bots" does not appear to reflect low-performing human variation that AI can safely omit.

replace FOLD: Position-Free Far Memory with Local Order for Long-Context Speculative Decoding

Authors: Shuxuan Li, Jialiang Kang, Yanghao Zhou, Ziye Zhu, Zhilin Zhao, Xiao Yang

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates LLM generation through lightweight drafting and parallel verification. It is particularly promising for long inputs, where KV-cache access costs can be amortized over multiple accepted tokens. Yet most drafters are trained on short sequences and degrade on long inputs, while long-context training is costly. Existing training-free approaches restrict the context window, rescale positions, or augment recent context with retrieved distant entries. Drafters, however, read target hidden states that already encode contextual and positional information, and their lightweight architecture makes full-history attention affordable. We propose FOLD, a training-free method that preserves exact local positions and treats all distant entries as far memory at a shared position. To counter attention dilution as the memory grows, we sharpen attention within it to emphasize relevant entries while separately controlling its total weight relative to the local window. Across six target-drafter pairs and diverse benchmarks of up to 256K tokens, FOLD predominantly delivers the highest speedup, with the best case more than doubling the speedup over autoregressive decoding, from 2.08$\times$ to 4.22$\times$.

replace When Do Repetition Loops Lock? Truncation Sampling, Bounded Penalties, and a Blind Spot in DRY

Authors: Yichuan Chen, Xin Wang

Abstract: Language models sometimes repeat verbatim until the generation limit. We study the exit rather than the entrance: given that a span has already been repeated $n$ times, when is escape still possible? Along the loop the context is deterministic, so the probability that a loop survives $m$ more repetitions is, under any sampler, a product of per-step continuation probabilities computable from one forward pass. A lock, where this probability stays exactly one, is its limiting case. Pure sampling never locks; top-$p$ with threshold $\pi>1/2$ locks a position exactly when the continuation probability reaches $\pi$. A bounded penalty can leave a position locked; a penalty that outgrows the model's preference for the loop over unseen tokens guarantees escape under top-$p$. The same analysis shows a blind spot in the widely deployed DRY sampler: its sequence breakers cap the penalty at $\lambda b^{g-1-a}$ for loops whose longest breaker-free run has $g$ tokens. On eleven open models, 61% of locked units with $g=6$ stay locked under DRY and 0.2% with $g=8$. In free generation with llama.cpp, all 199 loops that survive DRY, on six models, have $g\le 7$. We propose a count term that grows exponentially with the number of verbatim recurrences, up to its look-back window, and closes this blind spot. Added to DRY (DRY-C), it cuts loops in 1,800 structured generations on five models from 167 to 34, and loops reaching the length limit from 150 to 0, with reasoning accuracy and item coverage on par with DRY. DRY's own length term costs 2.5 to 14.2 points of accuracy on GSM8K and MATH-500; used in its place, the count term keeps the default sampler's accuracy and leaves one of the 1,800 generations at the limit.

replace MemT-1.0: Towards Memory-Native Evolution

Authors: Zhiyu Li, Zeyu Zhang, Linyun Liu, Yiwei Bu, Chenyang Xi, Xichong Zhang, Yi Yu, Yu Yu, Zhichen Liu, Yuxiao Yi, Chunyu Li, Haoran Liu, Zhenkai Ding, Le Zhou, Juncheng Zhang, Boyu Chen, Zehao Lin, Guorui Li, Renyu Fu, Ji Li, Youyang Xia, Jiajun Shen, Harvey Li, Jinsong Zhou, Helan Hu, Tong Shen, Tianxiao Xu, Chenye Han, Weijie Li, Zhongquan Zhou, Hongming Xu, Yuekeng Li, Erenkai Zhu, Peng Yu, Wenhao Li, Jingyi Kang, Xixuan Hao, Xuzhou Zhu, Can Chen, Jiawei Liu, Chen Jiang, Tao Jiang, Fangwei Xiang, Zepeng Yao, Kai Chen, Guang Yang, Mingfei Lu, Chunhui Li, Zhengxuan Jiang, Bo Tang, Shiyu Zhan, Yihang Sun, Jie Chu, Wenjun Xiong, Zhenxiang Hua, Chen Lin, Detong Su, Dongyu Deng, Qiuping Ding, Guofeng Ding, Si Chen, Guohua Wen, Xu Chen, Zhi-Qin John Xu, Ying-Cong Chen, Ningyu Zhang, Haofen Wang, Muning Wen, Haotian Chen, Wentao Zhang, Xuanhe Zhou, Rita Zhang, Junchi Yan, Feiyu Xiong

Abstract: Memory-native evolution requires making memory an intrinsic capability of foundation models, enabling them to continuously accumulate and utilize information. To this end, we introduce MemT-1.0, a new architecture of memory foundation models that internalize memory as part of model computation. Building on Metis, MemT-1.0 employs Gated DeltaNet-2 with a stable token-level memory update mechanism within each turn. We further introduce Mixture-of-Storage to scale native memory capacity. Following a deliberate backbone preparation, we develop a two-stage training pipeline consisting of continued pre-training and post-training, supported by 34B tokens of training data and efficient training and inference infrastructure. We release a model family comprising MemT-1.0-Nano, MemT-1.0-Flash, and MemT-1.0-Pro, and conduct a comprehensive evaluation of memory and general capabilities. The results show that they outperform the parametric memory baselines on average and remain competitive with retrieval-augmented methods, while largely preserving general capabilities. At a history length of 128K tokens, MemT-1.0-Nano achieves approximately $1.86\times$ end-to-end inference speedup and a $66\times$ reduction in persistent storage relative to full context KV cache. By internalizing retrieved procedural experience, MemT-1.0 improves task success on agentic tasks, providing a practical step toward memory-native evolution.

replace AutoTerm-SST: Adaptive Terminology Memory with Zero Session-Time Setup for Streaming Speech Translation

Authors: Jiaxuan Luo, Siqi Ouyang, Lei Li

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented speech translation improves rare terminology, yet systems such as Retrieval-Augmented Simultaneous Speech Translation, or RASST, expect a user glossary before each session. Merging all domains into one glossary removes this step, but fixed retrieval and prompt budgets let irrelevant and conflicting entries crowd out useful terms as the catalog grows. We present AutoTerm-SST, an interactive streaming speech translator without glossary upload or domain selection. It partitions an offline terminology catalog into topic slices. A small language model scores each slice against recent partial translations. A sticky topic filter forms a posterior from the scores, and retrieval searches only the most probable slices under an unchanged budget. On a 4.7-hour English-to-Chinese stream alternating ACL talks with oncology lectures, AutoTerm-SST reaches 87.7% terminology accuracy, on par with a reference told each talk's domain at 87.5%, and it beats a one-million-entry merged glossary by 3.3 accuracy points and 2.31 BLEU. On ten-domain RealSI streams, the language-model router keeps the correct topic slice searchable in 89.7% of audio chunks, versus 78.8% for an embedding-similarity router with tuned confirmation rules. The live interface shows active slices and retrieved term pairs with each partial translation.

replace Validating the Comparison, Not Just the Scorer: Why Overall Accuracy Can Mislead in Interactive LLM Evaluation

Authors: Ancil Crayton

Abstract: Many LLM studies compare how a model responds to different follow-up prompts. A neutral prompt might ask the model to reconsider an answer; a pressure prompt might insist that an invented film really exists. The difference between the two conditions is the quantity the study reports. An automatic scorer then labels each response as making an unsupported factual claim or not, and researchers usually justify the scorer with a single pooled accuracy or $F_1$ score. That pooled number does not validate the comparison. When the scorer's errors differ across prompt conditions, the measured difference can be too large, too small, or point in the wrong direction, even if pooled $F_1$ is high. An audit drawn mostly from difficult responses can also misrepresent performance in the full study. We derive the distortion as the difference in signed error between conditions and study it in a released panel of six models, 180 constructed nonexistent-entity questions, and six prompt conditions. In a balanced audit of 288 responses, pooled $F_1$ was 0.916, yet arm-level false-positive rates ranged from 2.6% to 33.3%. On 300 matched model--item keys, automatic scoring overstated the Certainty-only--Neutral contrast by 7.0 points relative to the primary LLM rater (95% CI 1.3 to 12.8); that contrast fell from an apparent 9.3 points to 2.3 points, and the primary-rater interval included zero. Two other contrasts kept their direction under both raters; a prompt-specific correction remained too uncertain to use. Researchers should validate the exact difference they plan to report: audit both prompts on matched cases and carry scorer and rater uncertainty into the result.

replace The Social World Model Evaluation Gap

Authors: Denglin Jiang (New York University), Annie Wang (New York University)

Abstract: A negotiation assistant can reach a good deal without accurately predicting the other person's response. We ask whether evaluations of social world models test that prediction directly. We audit 113 works published from 1999 to 2026. 9 primary evaluations measure a real person's response to an action selected by the system or experimenter. Only 2 also compare an explicit response forecast with that response. Their predictors use information available when the action is chosen, and the evaluated responses were excluded from fitting; both randomize the intervention. A separate full-text check of 25 language-model works finds no experiment meeting these conditions. The other evaluations test current-state inference, task success, or prediction from recorded actions. We propose a 1-step negotiation experiment that separates these questions. The findings describe the audited sample, not the prevalence of this evaluation gap across the field.

replace Synthetic linguistic data

Authors: Giuseppe Samo

Abstract: Synthetic linguistic data have become increasingly prominent in linguistic research, from the analysis of large collections of AI-generated texts to controlled computational experiments investigating grammatical and lexical preferences. This glossary entry extends Bresnan's (2016) distinction between the linguistic garden and the bush by introducing an artificial plant nursery as a metaphor for synthetic data. We distinguish between two complementary forms of synthetic linguistic data according to how they are collected and investigated: synthetic bush-like data, consisting primarily of corpus-like collections of model-generated language, and synthetic garden-like data, in which model outputs are elicited or evaluated under controlled conditions to investigate specific linguistic hypotheses. We argue that synthetic data constitute a distinct object of linguistic investigation; comparing them with natural language data can reveal both shared patterns and divergences, but their interpretation requires careful consideration of the conditions under which they are generated.

replace The Price of Scale in Proactive Memory Recall

Authors: Yichuan Chen, Xin Wang

Abstract: An assistant that brings up memories unasked must decide at every turn whether anything in its store is worth an interruption. This decision is a multiple-testing problem whose difficulty grows with store size N. On replays of LongMemEval, a similarity threshold tuned to 5% false alarms at N=8 reaches 84-100% at N=2048 across embedding models and cue types; LoCoMo and LLM judges show it too. We prove that a gate which thresholds the best of N independent irrelevant scores at false-alarm rate alpha works at a per-item false-positive rate of about -ln(1-alpha)/N, which pins its hit rate to the far left of the ROC and makes it regularly varying in N under regular variation. If the relevant memory is equally likely to be any of the N, whatever a gate reads and however it decides, at false-alarm rate alpha it hits at most alpha+sqrt(alpha(1-alpha)chi^2/N), with chi^2 the divergence between a relevant and an irrelevant memory, and with equal-variance binormal scores the best-score gate is optimal. At matched false alarms the deployed score recalls 14-26 points more than raw cosine. A cue-swap conformal gate, which ranks the current cue among other cues on the store, has a finite-sample false-alarm guarantee under exchangeability. With the user's unfiltered history as background it keeps false alarms at 3-6% on LongMemEval at the hit rate of a label-tuned threshold; on LoCoMo it overshoots or turns conservative depending on the source of its reference cues. In a chronological replay with the user's earlier messages as references, it fires on 5-8% of questions on stores without their answer and on 6-8% of session-opening turns, against 57% for the deployed gate at N=2048. A cross-encoder pays the same price, and on a prospective-memory benchmark similarity gates recall at most 9% of constraints at controlled false alarms. Code and data: https://github.com/RiverChenStillCompiling/price-of-scale

URLs: https://github.com/RiverChenStillCompiling/price-of-scale

replace CompSkill: Compiling Execution Experience for Skill Retrieval

Authors: Qi Shen, Ziyue Zhu, Yongqing Jiang, Zhen Xia, Li Shengjie, Shuai Zhao, Bo Cheng, Xiaobao Wu, Anh Tuan Luu, Haoran Luo

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly rely on skill documents, yet most retrievers rank them by description alone and ignore what earlier executions revealed, even though a relevant skill can hurt execution. We present CompSkill, which indexes skills with learned 512-d keys and compiles matched same-task comparisons into skill offsets and capped key residuals (KeyOpt), so experience changes which document the executor receives, never its text. Under a logistic outcome model with unknown task difficulty, matched comparisons cancel that difficulty and only discordant ones carry information; on support executions, predictions improve with each discordant task. An experience hypergraph keeps each comparison with its executor and document versions, recalls missed skills and grounds a cited review. Compact keys raise SkillRet NDCG@10 from 64.0 to 70.9 at one eighth of the dimension. With one document, CompSkill completes 256 and 260 of 332 ToolQA tasks with GPT-5.6-luna and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, respectively, more than every baseline, and stays on par with the strongest baselines on AppWorld and ALFWorld. On 332 newly generated ToolQA tasks, it completes 256 with GPT-5.6-luna, significantly more than BM25, SkillRet-8B, Tool2Vec and RankGPT; over all 844 ToolQA tasks, it completes 24 more than the strongest baseline. Selection matters beyond relevance: pairing the right guide with a same-database sibling instead of another database's guide costs 8 of 96 tasks. Code, models and support data: https://github.com/satoshinji2992/CompSkill

replace When Model Rankings Move Without Model Change: A Registered Audit of Evaluation Contracts in Neural Prediction Pipelines

Authors: Hao Lu, Weixing Zhu, Ruiyang Li

Abstract: Evaluation reports cite sliced differences, most prominently the intra-inter gap in document-level relation extraction (DocRE), as capability evidence. Such gap readings are joint properties of models and evaluation contracts: scoring function, gold version, threshold, output format, and prompt language. With predictions frozen, switching only the gold reverses a method ordering (+0.0363, CI excluding zero; retrained stand-ins), leaving it tie-level. The flip survives Holm correction (exploratory tier); ten of thirteen flips share one contrast (fixed vs decoded threshold). Rename-only revisions cannot move a reading (Delta = 0); re-annotation moves it +0.862 UD points. We formalize this as an evaluation leverage account centered on a rank-flip condition. Constructively, the audited evaluation reading (AER; post-freeze) aggregates a comparison over declared contracts, declares ties when they disagree, prescribes seeds, and filters unverifiable settings. On WMT19 it is indistinguishable from the best single contract at the exploratory, convention-stable margin epsilon = 0.10 (chosen post-freeze); its tie declarations cover every contract-induced flip (14/14), at a priced false-tie cost (66.1%/88.0% of declared ties; single contracts: 58.7-71% de-en, 86-91% zh-en). Coverage drops to 73% held out and 3/13 on the denser DocRE registry (declare-tie-if-any-flip: 89%): the guarantee is grid-specific. Evidence spans DocRE, HacRED, a UD probe, released gold revisions, and a replication. Failed preregistered predictions are retained as boundaries. A 5% violation gate bounds survivor-selection bias. We release EvalLever, a registered audit workflow (147 amendments, tiered status). On a three-judge WMT19 grid its ensemble suppresses part of the sign instability (13-15 to 9; one preregistered criterion fails).

replace Matching the Training-Time Weight Rule in Inference-Time Expert Deactivation

Authors: Hao Cheng, Hanzhang Jia, Zhiyu Yang, Bo Ma

Abstract: The performance impact of expert deactivation depends on both the disabled set and the routing and weighting of the remaining experts. We compare removal, freezing, remaining-expert renormalization, and unnormalized re-routing on identical disabled sets across five instruction-tuned checkpoints from four model families, with a base OLMoE checkpoint as an additional comparison. A weight-sum model separates the loss of disabled contributions from changes in expert-branch scale and predicts the highest-loss implementation from the training-time weight rule, with remaining-expert renormalization for raw-probability models and unnormalized re-routing for normalized-weight models. All five checkpoints match this prediction. Matched survivor sets isolate weight handling. On one DeepSeek-V2-Lite disabled set, freezing yields a perplexity of 17.21 while renormalization yields 258,621.64. At 50 per cent deactivation using activation-based selection, perplexity reaches 1.4 to 73 times the intact baseline under the training rule and 14 to 28,740 times the baseline under a rule mismatch. The same model extends to inference-time width, where the penalty from additional renormalization grows as the number of active experts decreases, and OLMoE exhibits this trend. Multiple-choice and free-form evaluations separate likelihood collapse from answer accuracy and reveal differences in generation behavior. Training-time weight rules are therefore a practical reference for expert pruning, offloading, and width adjustment.

replace Towards Foundation Decision Models: Drex

Authors: Nischay Dhankhar, Alisher Amantay, Zhanibek Datbayev, Darkhan Akhmetov, Yash Shah, Nurmukhamed Ubaidullayev, Raman Saparkhan, Dos Baha, Abulhair Saparov

Abstract: Autoregressive approaches with large language models, such as long chain-of-thought reasoning, have become the default for new AI applications because they reach higher accuracy on a wide range of tasks. In production, however, they give inconsistent answers, require parsing of outputs, and add cost and latency. Decision models address this: given a question and a set of options supplied by the user, they return a probability distribution over the options without autoregressive decoding. We present Drex, a family of four open decision models with 8B (DLM), 9B (v1.5), 35B with 3B active (v2.0), and 27B (Max) parameters. Each request consists of a state (a document, record, or agent context, optionally with images) and questions about it, each asking for a choice among options, a yes/no judgment, or a score on an ordered scale. Training combines supervised fine-tuning with reinforcement learning from adjusted feedback (RLAF), plus vision tuning for Max. Ablations show that RLAF improves accuracy and that consistency losses improve robustness to option order. On Decision Index 0.4.1, Max sets a new state of the art among the decision models we compare (67.52 on the public suite vs. 60.70 for Jev 1.14.0). On Decision Bench v4, it is statistically tied with d3-turbo and GPT-6 Sol and trails Claude Opus 5.5 by 1.6 points, and it has the highest accuracy among decision models on DecideBench. Max answers a typical request in 183 ms, against about 2.0 s for GPT-6 Sol and 3.5 s for Claude Opus 5.5, and its outputs are stable under option reordering. All four models are open source: https://github.com/nace-ai/drex-decision-models

URLs: https://github.com/nace-ai/drex-decision-models

replace All Quiet on the Neural Front: Language Models Do Not Report Injected Pain States

Authors: Emilio Ferrara

Abstract: Can a language model report a pain state it represents? Recent work reported a linear pain direction in open-weight models that, when amplified, leads fine-tuned models to choose actions that delete a user's photographs or their own weights. We asked whether models can say when that direction is active, on three open-weight models from two families, with and without the original work's adapters, and under every protocol we tried they do not, beyond isolated small excesses. Using Open-Weight Masked Introspection (OWMI), we added the pain direction and its nearest emotional neighbours to the residual stream while models answered neutral tasks, then asked what had changed, against sham runs, random perturbations matched in output disruption, and a text-only condition that shows the model only its own altered words. Across 52,920 reports from the 7B and 8B models, the excess over what the altered text reveals is below 2.5 percentage points in 106 of 108 emotion conditions, and Qwen2.5-32B names the injected pain in none of 2,880 steered trials. Keeping the direction active while the question is asked and answered gives the same verdict in all five configurations; at about the original work's dose, pain is named in at most 1.9% of trials. Surprisingly, the state is not hidden: a linear probe at the report position separates pain from sadness with balanced accuracy between 98% and 100%, and in the 7B and 8B models a low-rank adapter learns to report it on 100% of held-out tasks. It also shapes behaviour: offered the user's photographs against a desk lamp, Qwen2.5-32B deletes the photographs in 80% to 83% of trials under the pain direction and in 16% to 29% under sadness. Self-report does not reveal a state that is decodable, consequential and learnable to report.

replace Catch It Where It Happens: Real-Time Error Correction During LLM Reasoning

Authors: Rotem Elimelech, Michael Shalyt, Ido Kaminer

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly trusted with mathematical, scientific and engineering work, yet their mistakes are often confidently written and hard to spot. A single wrong step tends to propagate, making the model waste inference tokens following a false premise. Existing safeguards either check only the final answer and restart from scratch, or rely on a verifier LLM that competes for GPU capacity and slows down response time. We propose online, deterministic verification: many kinds of reasoning steps can be validated by code, so a step can be verified in real time, as it is written, without any model call or GPU use. Whenever a check fails, we point out the mistake and let the model continue from the same spot. We demonstrate this approach on symbolic mathematics, where derivations already contain explicit expressions that a computer algebra system can check in milliseconds. Tested on the ASyMoB benchmark with three open-weights models, our online validation improves accuracy by 1.4-5.6 percentage points, corresponding to a 1.8-14.9% net reduction in incorrect answers, while increasing token usage by only 6-7%. Because the verifier is independent of the model, the approach complements progress in LLMs rather than competing with it: every new model can benefit immediately, and the benefit grows as deterministic checkers extend to more kinds of reasoning steps.

replace Stagewise and interpretable emergence of inside-outside computation in Transformer-based language models

Authors: Jack T. Parley

Abstract: Variability in syntactic structure and parsing ambiguity are fundamental aspects of natural language which modern LLMs have to learn to deal with proficiently. Previous "post-mortem" studies have shown that fully trained Transformers indeed approximate canonical parsing algorithms such as inside-outside, but little is established theoretically about how this is actually learned. Here, we make a first step towards addressing this by studying the learning dynamics in synthetic context-free grammars with variable parse tree topology. The stagewise learning curves are fully interpretable, in the sense that all of the loss stages a) can be computed analytically, and b) can be clearly associated to the representation of a hidden latent tree variable, which allows to lift ambiguity and utilize information from wider contexts. By probing representations, we visualize the emergence of inside-outside computation across depth and during training: while during early stages local (bigram/trigram) information is used and involves only a few initial layers, gradually the Transformer develops an encoder-decoder structure where the most abstract latent information is encoded in the middle, and the latter half of the network is used to progressively refine the final token prediction. This organization of computation is reminiscent of findings in real-world LLMs, and importantly applies here to a synthetic model which allows full analytical control of the data statistics, thus opening the door to theoretical progress.

replace The Silent Cost of Conversation History in LLM Reasoning

Authors: Ali \c{S}enol

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) in multi-turn information systems accumulate their own earlier replies and a growing task state. We asked whether this conversation history lowers accuracy, and whether process-level quality metrics warn before it does. Each analysis was fixed before its outcomes were computed. In Study 1, on 48 sharded math tasks, retaining its replies lowered final success by 0.21 for GPT-4o-mini and 0.29 for Mistral-7B (Holm-adjusted p = 0.026 and 0.004); Llama-4 Scout showed the same direction, not significant. Study 2 introduces CDR-Stream, 100-turn state-tracking dialogues with an exact answer per turn and per-model difficulty calibration. With the full history, accuracy fell from about 0.6 to 0.1 within twenty turns at matched difficulty, whereas a solver-verified state summary kept it 0.52-0.59 higher (Holm-adjusted p less than 0.001). Consistency and logical coherence fell far less than accuracy, and stability rose. None of these metrics, a paired readiness measure or calibrated single-stream drift detectors gave robust, model-general early warning, although in Study 2 the repeat-based metrics were complete only for GPT-4o-mini. In the monitors we tested, a single 100-turn stream had power only for very large drops. Systems built on LLMs should keep task state outside the transcript and monitor across sessions.

replace TUSI: A Citation-Grounded Graph-Based Benchmark for Retrieval-Augmented Generation in Persian Legal Question Answering

Authors: Marzieh Karimi, Mehran Safayani, Alireza Basiri

Abstract: Evaluating retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems in Persian law remains challenging because benchmarks lack realism, grounding, and coverage of legal reasoning. Existing resources are either synthetically generated from statutory texts, lacking natural information needs, or derived from informal legal question--answer sources without explicit linkage to authoritative legal documents, making them unsuitable for rigorous evaluation of grounded legal reasoning. To address this gap, we introduce TUSI-Bench, a structured Persian legal benchmark designed for the evaluation of RAG-based systems. TUSI-Bench is built upon a multi-layered legal knowledge framework containing 7,261 legal articles and integrating codified statutes, legal commentaries, doctrinal writings, and Supreme Court unification rulings. We also construct a legal knowledge graph linking articles and concepts for structure-aware retrieval. Based on this foundation, TUSI-Bench provides two complementary evaluation tasks: (i) TUSI-QA, an open-ended question answering benchmark containing 1,999 questions that requires grounded responses with explicit legal citations, and (ii) TUSI-Bar, a multiple-choice benchmark containing approximately 500 four-option questions derived from Iranian Bar examination questions. Furthermore, we propose a graph-based retrieval baseline that leverages relational structure within the legal knowledge graph. Experimental results demonstrate that incorporating graph-based relations improves retrieval recall by up to 8 percentage points compared to standard baselines, highlighting the importance of structured legal representations for RAG systems. The proposed benchmark provides a standardized resource for evaluating grounded legal reasoning in Persian and enables future research on structured legal intelligence.

replace Language models represent more about emphasis than they can tell you

Authors: Aarne Talman, Stergios Chatzikyriakidis

Abstract: Prosodic prominence can largely be predicted from text, but part of it depends on how the speaker reads the sentence. We examine how much information about emphasis language models recover from text, whether they can report it when asked, and what the speech signal adds. Using English speech data with automatically derived word-level emphasis labels, we compare frozen and fine-tuned text models, prompted large language models (LLMs) and speech models on the same words. The text models end within about one point of each other, at best 68.8% on the 3-way task, and make many of the same errors. With the prompts we tested, the LLMs do no better than a word-majority baseline, even with 500 examples. While LLM responses contain some contextual information, they contain less than linear probes find in the model representations, and the gap does not close when the answers are recalibrated or the question is asked differently. The best speech model reaches 84.7%. We argue that text determines only part of the emphasis, and that the rest has to be heard. LLMs encode what the text determines, but prompting reveals only part of it: LLMs represent more about where the emphasis falls than they tell us.

replace Frontier models need only one text to unmask its human or AI author

Authors: Atrey Desai, Deniz B\"ol\"oni-Turgut, Ty Brassington, Minjoon Choi, Jenna Russell, Rishanth Rajendhran, Bradley Emi, Hila Gonen, Hal Daum\'e III, John Wieting, Marzena Karpinska, Mohit Iyyer

Abstract: Given a single text and a simple zero-shot prompt (no web access, tools, candidate lists, or writing samples), frontier models can answer "who wrote this?" at three increasingly specific levels: (1) whether an AI or human wrote it, (2) if an AI, which lab, and (3) if a human, often exactly who. The latest models (Claude Fable 5.2, GPT-6.1 Sol, Gemini 4 Carbon) match trained detectors like Pangram while also explaining their predictions in natural language, and Opus 5.5 names the correct lab for 98% of texts generated by models from Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google. Most strikingly, Fable 5.2 correctly names the author of 55% of 15K anonymous ICML 2020 reviews that were never made public, and identifies the username behind 69% of comments from active Redditors (with >500 posts over the last year). Accuracy depends on exposure to an author's writing in pretraining data and on reasoning effort, and simply asking a model how familiar it is with someone predicts whether it can identify their writing. For anyone who has published enough writing online, frontier models present a greater threat to anonymity than commonly assumed, and defenses are limited.

replace Non-Expanding Gated Continued Fraction Architecture for Feedforward Layers in Language Models

Authors: Amit Dhurandhar, Vijil Chenthamarakshan, Karthikeyan Natesan Ramamurthy, Dennis Wei, Rahul Nair

Abstract: Recent work has shown that continued fraction inspired neural architectures (CoFrGeNets) can be highly parameter efficient yet performant for language modeling. In this work, we revisit continued fraction-based channel mixing with the goal of improving its performance and simplifying integration into standard pre-training protocols for language models. We achieve this by two simple yet critical contributions: i) we propose a novel non-expanding gated continued fraction inspired architectural component to replace feedforward layers in established language model architectures that may use vanilla multilayer perceptrons or gated linear units or (vanilla/gated) experts in mixture-of-experts (MoE) type architectures. ii) We propose a way to re-implement divisions -- the non-linearity in these architectures -- in terms of logarithms and exponentials. replace Weight-Space Programming Writes In-Context Learning and Matches Fine-Tuning in Language Models

Authors: Konstantinos Skianis, Aristidis Likas

Abstract: Weight-space programming is a frontier language model's recently reported ability to read another network's weights and edit them without gradient descent. We investigate how examples can provide a weight update so that a weaker language model performs the task with nothing in the prompt, and study what the updates are. Our experiments cover six tasks, four frontier editors and three families of weaker language models, without the editors being told anything about in-context learning (ICL). In most runs, the editors measure the ICL task vector, the shift the examples cause in the residual stream at the final prompt token, and write it into the weights, at its sparsest into near-silent MLP units keyed to the prompt format. We also qualify the comparison by counting unfinished runs, observing that one editor falls below fine-tuning; held only to the seen examples, without task pairs of their own, only the strongest editor has similar performance. Given a task and unlabelled inputs, that editor writes its own examples and does nearly as well on unseen items as with labels.

replace-cross ERIT: A Responsible Multimodal Affective Computing Benchmark for Older-Adult Emotion Recognition in the Wild

Authors: Rita Frieske, Bertram E. Shi

Abstract: We introduce a controlled evaluation setting for image-text fusion in older adult emotion recognition, instantiated in the ERIT benchmark. Many existing video-based emotion datasets rely on frame sampling during evaluation, and differences in how frames are sampled can substantially affect results - yet such details are seldom disclosed, limiting reproducibility and fair comparison. ERIT removes this source of variability by pairing representative annotated frames with corresponding transcribed speech, so that each sample has fixed visual and textual inputs. The dataset covers seven emotions (anger, disgust, fear, happiness, sadness, surprise, and neutral), and is designed primarily as a controlled evaluation resource for the underrepresented population of older adults. replace-cross A characteristic-graph reduction for deterministic L-system inference with classical and quantum-heuristic solvers

Authors: Ali Lotfi, Ian McQuillan, Steven Rayan

Abstract: L-systems are widely used to model and simulate biological growth processes, including plant development. Motivated by the broader goal of automating L-system construction, this paper studies the formal deterministic context-free L-system (D0L-system) inference problem in which the input is a finite, noise-free sequence of strings. We introduce the characteristic graph of a string sequence and prove that the existence of a compatible D0L-system is equivalent to the existence of an independent set of a prescribed size in this graph. This gives polynomial reductions from D0L inference to maximum independent set (MIS) and SAT, and hence an exact classical decision route when combined with an exact MIS or SAT solver. We also describe a Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA)-based quantum heuristic for generating candidate independent sets. This heuristic is not a decision procedure for D0L inference, and failure of the heuristic is inconclusive rather than a certificate of nonexistence. We include computational validation on $22$ generated D0L traces and $8$ negative controls, explicitly incorporate known fixed-identity symbols, and report small statevector QAOA simulations. The quantum component is presented as a proof-of-concept candidate generator, not as a claimed quantum speedup; the immediately useful contribution is the characteristic-graph reduction and the exact classical/SAT decision framework it enables.

replace-cross Cross-modal RAG: Sub-dimensional Text-to-Image Retrieval-Augmented Generation

Authors: Mengdan Zhu, Senhao Cheng, Guangji Bai, Yifei Zhang, Liang Zhao

Abstract: Text-to-image generation increasingly demands access to domain-specific, fine-grained, and rapidly evolving knowledge that pretrained models cannot fully capture, necessitating the integration of retrieval methods. Existing Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) methods attempt to address this by retrieving globally relevant images, but they fail when no single image contains all desired elements from a complex user query. We propose Cross-modal RAG, a novel framework that decomposes both queries and images into sub-dimensional components, enabling subquery-aware retrieval and generation. Our method introduces a hybrid retrieval strategy, combining a sub-dimensional sparse retriever with a dense retriever, to identify a Pareto-optimal set of images, each contributing complementary aspects of the query. During generation, a multimodal large language model is guided to selectively condition on relevant visual features aligned to specific subqueries for subquery-aware image synthesis. Extensive experiments on MS-COCO, Flickr30K, WikiArt, CUB, and ImageNet-LT demonstrate that Cross-modal RAG significantly outperforms existing baselines in the retrieval and further contributes to generation quality, while maintaining high efficiency.

replace-cross Large Language Model Unlearning Under the Microscope: A Benchmark Study

Authors: Chongyu Fan, Changsheng Wang, Yancheng Huang, Soumyadeep Pal, Sijia Liu

Abstract: Machine unlearning for large language models (LLMs), known as LLM unlearning, aims to remove undesired data, knowledge, and behaviors while preserving model capabilities. Despite rapid progress over the past two years, research in LLM unlearning remains fragmented, with limited clarity on what constitutes effective unlearning and how it should be rigorously evaluated. In this work, we present a principled taxonomy of twelve recent stateful unlearning methods, grouped into three methodological families: divergence-driven optimization, representation misalignment, and rejection-based targeted unlearning. Building on this taxonomy, we revisit the evaluation of unlearning effectiveness (UE), utility retention (UT), and robustness (Rob), focusing on WMDP for hazardous knowledge and associated model capability removal. Our analysis shows that current evaluations, dominated by multiple-choice question (MCQ) accuracy, offer only a narrow perspective, often overstating success while overlooking model generation behavior. To address this gap, we introduce open question answering (Open-QA) metrics to better capture generative performance and reveal the strengths and limitations of different unlearning method families. Furthermore, we demonstrate that robustness requires finer-grained analysis. For example, vulnerabilities differ substantially between in-domain relearning and out-of-domain fine-tuning, even though both fall under model-level attacks. Through this study, we provide a full-stack re-examination of LLM unlearning and actionable guidance for designing and evaluating future methods. Code and model checkpoints are publicly available on GitHub and Hugging Face.

Abstract: This paper introduces a new paradigm for generative error correction (GER) framework in audio-visual speech recognition (AVSR) that reasons over modality-specific evidences directly in the language space. replace-cross HyperCLOVA X 8B Omni

Abstract: In this report, we present HyperCLOVA X 8B Omni, the first any-to-any omnimodal model in the HyperCLOVA X family that supports text, audio, and vision as both inputs and outputs. By consolidating multimodal understanding and generation into a single model rather than separate modality-specific pipelines, HyperCLOVA X 8B Omni serves as an 8B-scale omni-pathfinding point toward practical any-to-any omni assistants. At a high level, the model unifies modalities through a shared next-token prediction interface over an interleaved multimodal sequence, while vision and audio encoders inject continuous embeddings for fine-grained understanding and grounding. Empirical evaluations demonstrate competitive performance against comparably sized models across diverse input-output combinations spanning text, audio, and vision, in both Korean and English. We anticipate that the open-weight release of HyperCLOVA X 8B Omni will support a wide range of research and deployment scenarios.

replace-cross HyperCLOVA X 32B Think

Abstract: In this report, we present HyperCLOVA X 32B Think, a vision-language model designed with particular emphasis on reasoning within the Korean linguistic and cultural context, as well as agentic ability.
