replace-cross What If TSF: Evaluating Scenario-Guided Multimodal Time Series Forecasting

Authors: Jinkwan Jang, Hyunbin Jin, Hyungjin Park, Kyubyung Chae, Taesup Kim

Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled time series forecasting to move beyond numerical observations and incorporate external information in multimodal settings. Such information can improve forecasting performance, but accuracy alone may not reveal whether models appropriately respond to it: a model may ignore relevant information, fail to distinguish scenarios with different implications, or overreact to irrelevant or weak signals. We introduce What If TSF (WIT), a benchmark for evaluating whether models effectively respond to future scenarios. WIT constructs controlled scenario sets by fixing the forecasting state while systematically varying future scenarios, enabling three complementary evaluations: Factual Comparison, which measures the predictive utility of factual scenario information; Relational Comparison, which evaluates whether forecasts satisfy expected direction, contrast, intensity, and restraint relations across scenarios; and Grounded Comparison, which provides a complementary empirical assessment of scenario-induced revision trajectories where comparable real cases are available. Experiments show that factual accuracy gains do not consistently translate into appropriate responses to alternative scenarios, demonstrating the need to evaluate multimodal forecasting beyond predictive accuracy.

replace-cross Asymptotic Universal Alignment: A New Alignment Framework via Test-Time Scaling

Authors: Yang Cai, Weiqiang Zheng

Abstract: Aligning large language models (LLMs) to serve users with heterogeneous and potentially conflicting preferences is a central challenge for personalized and trustworthy AI. We formalize an ideal notion of universal alignment through test-time scaling: for each prompt, the model produces $k\ge 1$ candidate responses and a user selects their preferred one. We introduce $(k,f(k))$-robust alignment, which requires the $k$-output model to have win rate $f(k)$ against any other single-output model, and asymptotic universal alignment (U-alignment), which requires $f(k)\to 1$ as $k\to\infty$. Our main result characterizes the optimal convergence rate: there exists a family of single-output policies whose $k$-sample product policies achieve U-alignment at rate $f(k)=\frac{k}{k+1}$, and no method can achieve a faster rate in general. We show that popular post-training methods, including Nash learning from human feedback (NLHF), can fundamentally underutilize the benefits of test-time scaling. Even though NLHF is optimal for $k=1$, sampling from the resulting (often deterministic) policy cannot guarantee win rates above $\tfrac{1}{2}$ except for an arbitrarily small slack. This stems from a lack of output diversity: existing alignment methods can collapse to a single majority-preferred response, making additional samples redundant. In contrast, our approach preserves output diversity and achieves the optimal test-time scaling rate. In particular, we propose a family of symmetric multi-player alignment games and prove that any symmetric Nash equilibrium policy of the $(k+1)$-player alignment game achieves the optimal $(k,\frac{k}{k+1})$-robust alignment. Finally, we provide theoretical convergence guarantees for self-play learning dynamics in these games and extend the framework to opponents that also generate multiple responses.

replace-cross Beyond Forgetting: Representation Misdirection Elicits Controllable Side Behaviors and Capabilities

Authors: Tien Dang, The-Hai Nguyen, Dinh Mai Phuong, Nguyen Minh Phuong, Anh Bui, Hoang Thanh-Tung, Le-Minh Nguyen, Naoya Inoue

Abstract: We consider Representation Misdirection (RM), a simple unlearning mechanism for large language models (LLMs) that achieves unlearning by redirecting the latent representations of forget samples toward a target vector. Despite being important, the role of the target vector used in RM remains insufficiently explored. Here, we approach and revisit RM through the lens of the Linear Representation Hypothesis (LRH). Specifically, if one can identify a one-dimensional representation corresponding to a high-level concept, the LRH enables linear operations on this concept vector within the representation space of the forget samples. From this perspective, we hypothesize that, beyond forgetting, machine unlearning via RM elicits controllable side behaviors and capabilities corresponding to the high-level concept. We empirically validate this hypothesis across a wide range of concepts and tasks, including controlling unlearned models' truthfulness, sentiment, stereotypical bias, refusal, language, and instruction-following. Our findings reveal that this phenomenon could be either a hidden risk if misused or a mechanism that can be harnessed for developing unlearned models that require stronger capabilities and controllable behaviors.

replace-cross Towards Adaptive, Scalable, and Robust Coordination of LLM Agents: A Dynamic Ad-Hoc Networking Perspective

Authors: Rui Li, Zeyu Zhang, Xiaohe Bo, Quanyu Dai, Chaozhuo Li, Feng Wen, Xu Chen

Abstract: Multi-agent architectures built on large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated the potential to realize swarm intelligence through well-crafted collaboration. However, the substantial burden of manual orchestration inherently raises an imperative to automate the design of agentic workflows. We frame such an agent coordination challenge as a classic problem in dynamic ad-hoc networking: How to establish adaptive and reliable communication among a scalable number of agentic hosts? In response to this unresolved dilemma, we introduce RAPS, a reputation-aware publish-subscribe paradigm for adaptive, scalable, and robust coordination of LLM agents. RAPS is grounded in the Distributed Publish-Subscribe Protocol, allowing LLM agents to exchange messages based on their declared intents rather than predefined topologies. Beyond this substrate, RAPS further incorporates two coherent overlays: (i) Reactive Subscription, enabling agents to dynamically refine their intents; and (ii) Bayesian Reputation, empowering each agent with a local watchdog to detect and isolate malicious peers. Extensive experiments over five benchmarks showcase that our design effectively reconciles adaptivity, scalability, and robustness in a unified multi-agent coordination framework.

replace-cross RAM-Net: Linear-Time Sequence Modeling with Sparsely Addressable State

Authors: Kaicheng Xiao, Haotian Li, Liran Dong, Guoliang Xing

Abstract: Linear attention offers an efficient alternative to full attention with a fixed-size recurrent state. However, this state is shared by all tokens, so information from distinct tokens becomes superposed within it and produces inter-token interference that degrades long-range fine-grained recall. To address this issue, we propose RAM-Net, which replaces dense access to a shared state with sparse address-based access. RAM-Net organizes the recurrent state as a fixed-size array of independent slots and uses an Address Decoder that maps each key or query into a sparse address, selecting a small subset of slots to write to or read from at each step. This design directs tokens with non-overlapping addresses to disjoint slots, suppressing inter-token interference, while keeping per-step state access dependent only on the number of selected slots rather than the total state size. Empirically, RAM-Net outperforms strong recurrent baselines on fine-grained long-range retrieval and achieves the lowest perplexity with competitive commonsense reasoning. It does so while accessing fewer state elements per step than all baselines, e.g., $8\times$ fewer than Mamba2.

replace-cross Evaluating Small Open LLMs for Medical Question Answering: A Practical Framework

Authors: Avi-ad Avraam Buskila

Abstract: Incorporating large language models (LLMs) in medical question answering demands more than high average accuracy: a model that returns substantively different answers each time it is queried is not a reliable medical tool. Online health communities such as Reddit have become a primary source of medical information for millions of users, yet they remain highly susceptible to misinformation; deploying LLMs as assistants in these settings amplifies the need for output consistency alongside correctness. We present a practical, open-source evaluation framework for assessing small, locally-deployable open-weight LLMs on medical question answering, treating reproducibility as a first-class metric alongside lexical and semantic accuracy. Our pipeline computes eight quality metrics, including BERTScore, ROUGE-L, and an LLM-as-judge rubric, together with two within-model reproducibility metrics derived from repeated inference (N=10 runs per question). Evaluating three models (Llama 3.1 8B, Gemma 3 12B, MedGemma 1.5 4B) on 50 MedQuAD questions (N=1,500 total responses) reveals that despite low-temperature generation (T=0.2), self-agreement across runs reaches at most 0.20, while 87-97% of all outputs per model are unique -- a safety gap that single-pass benchmarks entirely miss. The clinically fine-tuned MedGemma 1.5 4B underperforms the larger general-purpose models on both quality and reproducibility; however, because MedGemma is also the smallest model, this comparison confounds domain fine-tuning with model scale. We describe the methodology in sufficient detail for practitioners to replicate or extend the evaluation for their own model-selection workflows. All code and data pipelines are available at https://github.com/aviad-buskila/llm_medical_reproducibility.

URLs: https://github.com/aviad-buskila/llm_medical_reproducibility.

replace-cross Beyond SFT-to-RL: Pre-alignment via Black-Box On-Policy Distillation for Multimodal RL

Authors: Sudong Wang, Weiquan Huang, Xiaomin Yu, Zuhao Yang, Hehai Lin, Keming Wu, Chaojun Xiao, Chen Chen, Wenxuan Wang, Beier Zhu, Yunjian Zhang, Chengwei Qin

Abstract: The standard post-training recipe for large multimodal models (LMMs) applies supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on curated demonstrations followed by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). However, SFT introduces distributional drift that neither preserves the model's original capabilities nor faithfully matches the supervision distribution. This problem is further amplified in multimodal reasoning, where perception errors and reasoning failures follow distinct drift patterns that compound during subsequent RL. We introduce PRISM, a three-stage pipeline that mitigates this drift by inserting an explicit distribution-alignment stage between SFT and RLVR. Building on the principle of on-policy distillation (OPD), PRISM casts alignment as a black-box, response-level adversarial game between the policy and a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) discriminator with dedicated perception and reasoning experts, providing disentangled corrective signals that steer the policy toward the supervision distribution without requiring access to teacher logits. While 1.26M public demonstrations suffice for broad SFT initialization, distribution alignment demands higher-fidelity supervision; we therefore curate 113K additional demonstrations from Gemini 3 Flash, featuring dense visual grounding and step-by-step reasoning on the hardest unsolved problems. Experiments on Qwen3-VL show that PRISM consistently improves downstream RLVR performance across multiple RL algorithms (GRPO, DAPO, GSPO) and diverse multimodal benchmarks, improving average accuracy by +4.4 and +6.0 points over the SFT-to-RLVR baseline on 4B and 8B, respectively. Our code, data, and model checkpoints are publicly available at https://github.com/XIAO4579/PRISM.

URLs: https://github.com/XIAO4579/PRISM.

replace-cross Unsolvability Ceiling in Multi-LLM Routing: An Empirical Study of Evaluation Artifacts

Authors: Saloni Garg, Amit Sagtani

Abstract: Efficient routing across multiple LLMs enables cost-quality tradeoffs by directing queries to the cheapest capable model. Prior work attributes routing headroom to an "unsolvability ceiling", queries no model in the pool can solve. We present a large-scale study of multi-tier LLM routing with 206,000 query-model pairs across six benchmarks (MMLU, MedQA, HumanEval, MBPP, Alpaca, ShareGPT) using the Gemma 4 and Llama 3.1 families. Evaluating with both LLM-as-a-judge and exact-match metrics, we show that a substantial portion of reported unsolvability stems from evaluation artifacts: (i) systematic judge biases favoring verbosity over correctness, (ii) truncation under fixed generation budgets, and (iii) output format mismatches. Through dual-judge validation and exact-match grounding, we reduce measured unsolvability across tasks. We introduce a decomposition framework attributing failures to these artifacts, revealing consistent patterns across domains and model families. These artifacts also distort router training signals: standard routers collapse to majority-class prediction (~79% smallest-tier optimal), confirmed via random-feature and shuffled-label controls, incurring a 13-17 percentage point opportunity cost. We provide actionable recommendations including dual-judge validation, exact-match anchoring, and cost-sensitive objectives. Our findings suggest existing routing headroom estimates are substantially inflated, underscoring the need for reliable evaluation protocols in multi-LLM systems.

Authors: Chengzhi Shen (Cherise), Weixiang Shen (Cherise), Tobias Susetzky (Cherise), Chen (Cherise), Chen (Cherise), Jun Li, Yuyuan Liu, Xuepeng Zhang, Zhenyu Gong, Daniel Rueckert, Jiazhen Pan

Abstract: Intensive care units (ICU) generate long, dense and evolving streams of clinical information, where physicians must repeatedly reassess patient states under time pressure, underscoring a clear need for reliable AI decision support. Existing ICU benchmarks typically treat historical clinician actions as ground truth. However, these actions are made under incomplete information and limited temporal context of the underlying patient state, and may therefore be suboptimal, making it difficult to assess the true reasoning capabilities of AI systems. We introduce RealICU, a hindsight-annotated benchmark for evaluating large language models (LLMs) under realistic ICU conditions, where labels are created after senior physicians review the full patient trajectory. We formulate four physician-motivated tasks: assess Patient Status, Acute Problems, Recommended Actions, and Red Flag actions that risk unsafe outcomes. We partition each trajectory with 30-min windows and release two datasets: RealICU-Gold with 930-window annotations from 94 MIMIC-IV patients, and RealICU-Scale with 11,862 windows extended by Oracle, a physician-validated LLM hindsight labeler. Existing LLMs including memory-augmented ones performed poorly on RealICU, exposing two failure modes: a recall-safety tradeoff for clinical recommendations, and an anchoring bias to early interpretations of the patient. We further introduce ICU-Evo to study structured-memory agents that improves long-horizon reasoning but does not fully eliminate safety failures. Together, RealICU provides a clinically grounded testbed for measuring and improving AI sequential decision-support in high-stakes care. Project page: https://chengzhi-leo.github.io/RealICU-Bench/

replace-cross To See is Not to Learn: Protecting Multimodal Data from Unauthorized Fine-Tuning of Large Vision-Language Models

Authors: Chengshuai Zhao, Zhen Tan, Dawei Li, Tianlong Chen, Zhiyuan Yu, Neil Zhenqiang Gong, Huan Liu

Abstract: The rapid advancement of Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) is increasingly accompanied by unauthorized scraping and training on multimodal web data, posing copyright and privacy risks to data owners. Existing countermeasures, such as machine unlearning and watermarks, are inherently post-hoc approaches that act only after intellectual property infringement has already occurred. In this work, we propose MMGuard to empower data owners to proactively protect their multimodal data against unauthorized LVLM fine-tuning. MMGuard generates unlearnable examples by injecting human-imperceptible perturbations that exploit the learning dynamics of LVLMs. By minimizing the training loss, the perturbation creates an optimization shortcut, causing the model to overfit to the noise and thereby degrading downstream performance when the perturbation is absent during inference. To further strengthen this defense, MMGuard introduces a cross-modal binding disruption, shifting LVLM attention to enforce a spurious correlation between the noise and the training target with theoretical guarantees. MMGuard is evaluated against nine open-source LVLMs across six datasets. Our results demonstrate effective, stealthy, and robust protection under white-box, gray-box, and no-box threat models, establishing a mechanistic advantage in proactively defending against unauthorized fine-tuning exploitation.

replace-cross A Tutorial on Diffusion Theory: From Differential Equations to Diffusion Models

Authors: Jiayi Fu, Yuxia Wang

Abstract: Diffusion models have emerged as a leading framework for generative modeling, yet their mathematical foundations are often presented in separate treatments. This tutorial offers a unified, self-contained introduction organized into three parts. First, we construct a forward generative stochastic differential equation (SDE) that transports noise to data, marginalize over the clean data endpoint, and derive training objectives directly from the forward dynamics. Second, we begin with a conditional path from data to noise, marginalize it to obtain the forward process, and derive the corresponding reverse-time SDE and training objectives. Within this forward-reverse framework, we also establish the connection between denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs) and variance-preserving (VP) SDEs. Third, we review numerical methods for solving SDEs and discuss two approaches to guided generation: classifier guidance and classifier-free guidance.

Authors: Oscar Chew, Serhii Honcharenko, Qian-Hui Chen, Patricia Lu, Dishant Zaveri, Khoa D. Doan, Kuan-Hao Huang

Abstract: A key capability for video understanding is reliably linking subjects to events across time, yet whether Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) actually achieve this remains unclear. In this work, we introduce DistractionBench to evaluate whether VideoLLMs can robustly link subjects and events in the presence of unrelated video segments. Through controlled interventions, such as inserting short advertisement clips into longer videos, we show that VideoLLMs frequently hallucinate interactions between entities from different segments, incorrectly attributing actions from injected advertisements to subjects in the main video. We characterize this systematic hallucination as bag-of-events (BoE) behavior, where models process videos as collections of events rather than temporally structured sequences. Evaluating 11 popular VideoLLMs, we find that all models exhibit substantial BoE behavior. Our findings suggest that VideoLLMs lack reliable mechanisms for temporal grounding and motivate the development of models with more robust subject-event association.

replace-cross Invisible to humans, visible to machines: a preregistered audit of Unicode fidelity across four biomedical bibliographic APIs

Authors: Przemys{\l}aw Czuma

Abstract: To develop a reusable, preregistered method for auditing the character-level fidelity of bibliographic application programming interfaces (APIs), and to apply it to four public APIs used to build biomedical text corpora. Preregistered audit (OSF osf.io/zjevr). From the 2024 PubMed Central (PMC) Open Access subset, a simple random sample of 4,000 English-language research articles was drawn. Abstracts returned by the PubMed E-utilities, Crossref, OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar were compared with the PMC Journal Article Tag Suite (JATS) XML abstract for 51 registered code points, 34 of them in four primary classes: typographic punctuation, mathematical/scientific symbols, Greek letters and special whitespace. A cell (API x class) was classified as systematic loss when the upper 95% Wilson bound of conditional preservation was below 5%, and as faithful when the lower bound exceeded 90%. The mechanism of each loss was examined in the aligned text and the raw API responses. Two of 16 cells met the loss criterion. PubMed preserved typographic punctuation in 0.6% of eligible abstracts (12/2,113; 95% CI 0.3-1.0%), replacing it with ASCII equivalents. OpenAlex preserved special whitespace in 0.0% (0/1,085; 95% CI 0.0-0.4%), which its inverted-index format cannot store. Mathematical symbols and Greek letters were preserved by all four APIs (95.2-99.7%). Crossref returned an abstract for 75.4% of papers (95% CI 74.1-76.7%) and for none published by Elsevier (0/360) or the American Chemical Society (0/105). The same published abstract reaches text-mining pipelines with different characters depending on the API that serves it. Studies that analyze characters, including character-level markers of writing assisted by large language models, should take text from a source shown to preserve those characters, or normalize all sources identically, and report the API and retrieval date.

Authors: Brian La, Sungbin Jo, Sejoon Chang, Ben Kim, Junyoung Bae, Aamish Ahmad Beg, Sei Chang, Gonzalo Gonzalez-Pumariega

Abstract: Coding agents face new challenges in game development, where they must produce code that is not only syntactically correct but also consistent with engine-managed lifecycle, state, and networking behavior. Across 11 model-harness configurations, the best-scoring configuration achieves 46.4% pass@1, while 43 tasks remain unsolved by every evaluated configuration. replace-cross When Simpler Is Better: Evaluating Translation Pipelines for Medieval Latin Manuscripts

Authors: Nguyen Kim Hai Bui, Md. Easin Arafat, Tam\'as G\'abor Orosz, Mufti Mahmud

Abstract: Despite remarkable progress in machine translation, Vision Language Models (VLMs) struggle on historical manuscripts, a domain that stresses core Natural Language Processing (NLP) capabilities: low-resource transliteration, archaic vocabulary, and noisy input signals. We present a systematic framework for evaluating the full image-to-translation pipeline on medieval Latin manuscripts, a setting in which scribal shorthand, ligatures, and parchment degradation expose failure modes that are invisible in clean-text benchmarks. Benchmarking on the CATMuS Latin dataset reveals a specialization gap: domain-specific Optical Character Recognition (OCR) models reduce character error rate by up to 4.3$\times$ compared to general-purpose VLMs, despite operating at orders of magnitude fewer parameters. Adding retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) or post-OCR correction introduces prompt saturation and error propagation that degrade aggregate translation quality. These findings offer both a new benchmark and practical guidance for deploying translation systems in low-resource historical settings.

replace-cross From Inference to Adaptation: Vision-Language Model Test-Time Adaptation via a Unified Optimal Transport View

Authors: Qi Yu, Zhichen Zeng, Katherine Tieu, Lingjie Chen, Xiyuan Yang, Ruizhong Qiu, Yuchen Yan, Lihui Liu, Yanjun Zhao, Ziao Wang, Lei Ying, Jingrui He, Hanghang Tong

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have demonstrated remarkable zero-shot capabilities yet remain sensitive to real-world distribution shifts. Although significant efforts are devoted to adapting VLMs at test time, they rely heavily on noisy pseudo-labels predicted from cross-modal embedding similarities during inference, which are unreliable under distribution shifts and mislead the adaptation. Moreover, existing methods largely overlook the critical relational structures across different modalities during test-time adaptation, whose supervision signals remain surprisingly robust under distribution shifts. In this work, we aim to jointly align the cross-modal semantics and intra-modal relational structures across modalities at test time to improve VLM adaptation. At the inference stage, we formulate the zero-shot image classification task as a cross-modal alignment problem via a Fused Gromov-Wasserstein (FGW) formulation, which considers both cross-modal semantics and intra-modal structures to generate robust pseudo-labels. At the adaptation stage, we propose a multi-level InfoNCE loss to leverage both the semantical and relational alignment supervision from the inference stage, empowering fine-grained adaptation of VLMs under distribution shifts with additional structural consistency. Finally, we bridge the inference and adaptation stages of our method by proving a mathematical equivalence between their objective functions, thereby revealing the mutual benefits of these two stages from a theoretical perspective. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness and efficiency of our methods, outperforming the best-performing methods by up to 3% while maintaining a superior balance between effectiveness and efficiency.

replace-cross Naive Prompt Optimization: Rethinking the Need for Complex Prompt Search

Authors: Yuan Chang, Xiaoqi Chen

Abstract: Efficiently improving autonomous agents across diverse tasks is central to accelerating recursive self-improvement (RSI) in agentic AI, with prompt optimization emerging as a promising approach capable of delivering performance gains comparable to those achieved by fine-tuning model weights, while reducing computational costs in both optimization and serving. However, recent developments increasingly favor unnecessarily complex prompt optimizers. We introduce Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO), a lightweight single-lineage method that iteratively revises prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback. NPO achieves comparable or better performance than GEPA with fewer rollouts in IFBench and HotpotQA, and its advantage increases with stronger teacher models, suggesting that stronger teacher reasoning may partially substitute for optimizer-side search complexity. Overall, our preliminary results show that simple, linear prompt optimization can rival substantially more sophisticated and complex search procedures.

replace-cross On the Recall Scaling Laws in Mamba: A Theoretical and Mechanistic Study via Hashing

Authors: Yuval Koren, Assaf Ben-Kish, Raja Giryes, Lior Wolf, Itamar Zimerman

Abstract: Associative Recall (AR) is the cognitive ability to learn and retrieve links between items in memory. In NLP, AR is used as a benchmark for evaluating the in-context memory capacity of architectures such as Mamba, and has been found to strongly correlate with language modeling performance. This paper explores AR from the perspective of mechanistic interpretability, aiming to reverse-engineer the exact internal algorithm used by Mamba to perform recall. Our key insight is that Mamba performs recall by implicitly learning linear hash functions, and we identify the low-level circuit that enables this behavior. Building on these findings and inspired by theoretical tools in similarity-preserving hashing, such as the Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma, we develop a theoretical framework for analyzing AR, which we term Recall Scaling Laws. Given the vocabulary size and the number of facts in context, this framework allows us to (1) predict the embedding and state dimensions required for Mamba to achieve perfect recall, (2) predict recall success probability given the model dimensions, and (3) analyze multi-layer models and multi-head SSM patterns. Empirical results show that our theoretical findings are accurate and predictive, offering insights into how AR capacity scales with vocabulary, state, embedding size, and architecture.

replace-cross Efficient Cost-Aware LLM Evaluation via Bayesian Bandit Gittins Indices

Authors: Qian Xie, Yueli He, Nairen Cao

Abstract: Exhaustively evaluating every candidate LLM configuration on every benchmark item to identify a high-performing one is costly. We formulate configuration selection as a cost-aware Bayesian bandit problem and propose GittinsEval, inspired by Bayesian-optimal Gittins-index policies for Markov chain selection. We augment this policy with a separate anytime recommendation rule over both fully and partially evaluated configurations, using an LCB-style score to account for posterior uncertainty. GittinsEval is computationally efficient, requiring only lightweight online updates after offline precomputation. Across tasks with varying numbers of candidates and examples, GittinsEval is consistently competitive, with particularly strong gains over the Bayesian optimization baselines on few-candidate large-example benchmarks and over cost-unaware frequentist baselines on large-candidate tasks. Crucially, GittinsEval often attains near-zero simple regret using only 1%-2% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost; it also offers a stopping rule that typically triggers at 1%-10% with an appropriate cost--quality tradeoff parameter.

replace-cross TraceDance: An Automated System for Building Agent Behavior Benchmarks from Real-World Agent Deployment Traces

Authors: Dehai Min, Daoan Zhang, Yiming Zeng, Huayi Zhang, Ziyi Chen, Yan Zhang, Qinbo Bai, Mengyuan Chao, Jing Ning, Qiyue Hua, Huiyi Chen, Hanrong Zhang, Henry Peng Zou, Jie Yang, Wei Xu, Philip S. Yu

Abstract: An agent can complete a task while exhibiting undesirable behavior during execution. Nine frontier LLMs achieve a mean pass rate of only 26.7%, showing that they still struggle to respond appropriately at the evaluated decision points. Analysis across behavior-specific benchmarks further reveals weaknesses in how current LLMs behave as agents. By turning deployment problems into targeted benchmarks, TraceDance could serve as a key component of the recursive self-improvement (RSI) loop.

replace-cross Surprising Success, Repeated Failure: Entropy-Guided Credit Assignment for Exploration in LLM Reasoning

Authors: Woongyeong Yeo, Minki Kang, Chanuk Lee, Sangwoo Park, Jinheon Baek, Sung Ju Hwang

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) enhances reasoning in large language models (LLMs) through outcome-level feedback, yet recent approaches to finer-grained credit assignment often require auxiliary models, additional sampling, or privileged information. Although policy entropy provides a readily available signal, prioritizing uncertain positions under both reinforcement and penalization concentrates penalties where failed responses still retain alternatives for recovery, which can suppress opportunities for exploration. To address this, we introduce Entropic Advantage Policy Optimization (EAPO), an entropy-guided credit assignment method that treats success and failure asymmetrically. Specifically, motivated by the observation that success under uncertainty is less repeatable while confident failures tend to recur, EAPO couples normalized policy entropy with the sign of the response advantage to reinforce surprising success and correct repeated failure. It assigns stronger reinforcement to high-entropy decisions in successful responses and stronger penalties to low-entropy decisions in failed responses, while attenuating penalties at uncertain positions to preserve opportunities for recovery. By redistributing the response advantage across tokens, EAPO derives token-level credit directly from existing rollout signals without additional supervision. We validate EAPO on a range of reasoning tasks across both base and reasoning backbones, demonstrating that it achieves the best overall performance. We further show that EAPO promotes more effective exploration, broadening problem coverage and diversifying successful reasoning strategies.

replace-cross Loop Dropout: Regularizing Shared Updates in Looped Language Models

Authors: Zirui Zhu, Hailun Xu, Xuanlei Zhao, Yong Liu, Yingxuan Ren, Kanchan Sarkar, Kun Xu, Yang You

Abstract: Looped language models separate computational depth from parameter count by repeatedly applying the same transformer block. Adapting these models requires a shared update that remains effective as hidden states evolve throughout the recurrent computation. Our empirical analysis reveals a pronounced late-loop bias in standard low-rank adaptation (LoRA): the shared update provides limited adaptation at early loop positions. This imbalance motivates training shared updates under varying combinations of their applications. Randomly omitting adapter applications alone, however, does not improve task performance; it reduces expected update strength during training while leaving inference unchanged. We introduce Loop Dropout, which couples stochastic masking of adapter applications with inverse-survival rescaling to preserve expected update strength and promote effective adaptation across loops. Extensive experiments demonstrate improved mathematical reasoning across model sizes, adapter ranks and training recipes, with benefits extending to general instruction tuning and code generation. Loop Dropout outperforms existing LoRA variants and adapter regularizers, while further analysis shows stronger early-loop adaptation. Every backbone loop remains active, and inference applies the adapter at all loops using standard LoRA without additional trainable parameters or inference computation. Code is available at https://github.com/NUS-HPC-AI-Lab/loop-dropout.

URLs: https://github.com/NUS-HPC-AI-Lab/loop-dropout.

replace-cross ArgGYM: A Procedural, Engine-Verified Benchmark for Structured Defeasible Reasoning

Authors: \.Ibrahim Ethem Deveci, Funda Tan \c{C}al{\i}k, Bar{\i}\c{s} Deniz Sa\u{g}lam, Duygu Ataman

Abstract: Recent progress in large language model reasoning has been driven by benchmarks and reinforcement learning environments with automatically verifiable rewards, particularly in mathematics, code, and formal logic. These settings make model accuracy easier to evaluate and optimize, but it remains unclear how far success under fixed problem specifications and stable evaluation criteria transfers to reasoning outside such domains. Real-world reasoning often proceeds under incomplete and revisable information: conclusions may be supported provisionally, defeated by counter-evidence, reinstated by further arguments, or revised when stronger reasons become available. Reasoning of this kind is generally referred to as defeasible reasoning. We introduce ArgGYM, a procedural benchmark and RLVR-compatible training environment for structured defeasible reasoning. ArgGYM decomposes this reasoning into twelve tasks and grounds task-specific scoring in a symbolic argumentation engine that computes the formal states used to evaluate model outputs. It includes a frozen benchmark of 1,440 verified instances across fifteen curriculum configurations, two argument preference orderings, and two set orderings, while the same generators and verifiers can produce fresh instances, reducing dependence on static test sets, for both evaluation and verifiable-reward training. On the frozen benchmark, frontier and open-weight models show sharply different reasoning profiles: they can recover substantial parts of structured answers without solving the complete task, and performance declines in later curriculum configurations with longer dependencies and more interacting structures. Training Qwen3.5-4B with ArgGYM's verifiers as rewards more than doubles complete-task success on held-out instances, with the largest gains on constructive tasks.

replace-cross FAER: A Negative-Result Audit of Offline Trajectory Replay Selection on GSM8K

Authors: Miaobo Hu, Shuhao Hu, Xiaobo Guo, Xin Wang, Bokun Wang, Tianshu Fu, Daren Zha

Abstract: Offline trajectory replay selects model-generated reasoning traces from a frozen cache for supervised fine-tuning (SFT), yet cache-side answer correctness need not predict downstream accuracy on unseen problems. We report a failure-retaining audit of offline response SFT on GSM8K with Qwen3-1.7B fp16 LoRA over a frozen 384-row cache (256 training rows and 128 excluded held-out rows). Across three seeds (13, 17, 23) on a 256-problem test split, all three 32-row replay rules (uniform, format-feedback, and feedback-aware) obtain mean strict accuracies (0.7786, 0.7682, and 0.7956) below the no-update base model (0.8008), and every paired 95% confidence interval spans zero. Selector ranking is post-hoc unidentified (3 completed selectors versus the repaired protocol's minimum of 5, with rank correlation spanning both signs). Under an 8,192-supervised-token windowed schedule, 9 of 12 runs halt under calibration drift or unresolved nonfinite training loss or gradients, leaving only full-cache replay completed at 0.7721. A repaired campaign with dynamic loss scaling halted at its learning-rate pilot under reference-solution loss drift before accessing the 346-problem locked test split. Because the gold-rationale positive control was never executed, these negative results cannot separate cache quality, selector quality, and learner-protocol limits. Our one positive finding separates parser repair from prompt changes on a 64-problem audit split: reparsing fixed base generations with a Decimal extractor raises parse rate from 0.8750 to 1.0000 and accuracy from 0.7344 to 0.8281, whereas appending a format suffix flips 0.2500 of canonical answers. A preregistered replication on 512 fresh GSM8K training problems reproduces this parser-only gain (+8.40 pp, 95% CI [+6.05, +10.94]) for this checkpoint. We contribute a ledger-traceable negative-result record and 9 lessons learned.

replace-cross RouteAudit: A Negative Result on Adaptive Multi-Verifier Routing under Matched Fixed-Subset Controls

Authors: Miaobo Hu, Shuhao Hu, Xiaobo Guo, Xin Wang, Bokun Wang, Tianshu Fu, Daren Zha

Abstract: Apparent savings from adaptive verifier routing can come from a fixed verifier subset. We audit GSM8K using cached Qwen3.5-0.8B or Qwen3.5-2B candidates paired with Qwen3-4B, three LLM verifiers, 1,319 held-out requests, and 5 router seeds. Our shared-fusion audit compares routers with the complete fixed-subset envelope, including zero-call abstention, under matched prices and scoring. Calibration-selected single-call verification matches full static verification in 3 of 4 cells. In a separate comparison using the original fusion rules and two-verifier catalog, archived learned and offline-RL routers lose 0.038 to 0.171 in quality against matched static SF+SA. Across 480 budget-matched offline fits in six router families, the family envelope gain is zero in each cell at the primary price. No locked router obtains a positive simultaneous bootstrap lower bound across 68 cell-by-price points. None is classified as complete-static dominant, and all 4 primary-price points are inconclusive. At the primary price, the tested-family gain upper bound is at most 0.002. The oracle headroom upper bound is 0.034 to 0.045, above our 0.01 margin, leaving meaningful gains open. At 45 of 64 grid points excluding the primary price, the oracle lower bound exceeds 0.01 and the tested-family upper bound is at most 0.01. Coverage of these envelope bounds remains unverified. At a true gain of 0.03, the procedure rejects on 44.1% of points, and planted-effect family coverage ranges from 0.923 to 0.931. We report lessons from this catalog.

replace-cross SkillForgeBench: Evaluating Agent Reliability Grounded in Agent Skill Specifications

Authors: Zhengxi Li, Junyi Zhou, Qiyuan Zhang, Chuhan Wu, Fuyuan Lyu, Le Tian, Xiao Zhou, Xue Liu, Chen Ma, Yufei Wang

Abstract: Evaluating an agent's ability to strictly satisfy diverse and procedurally complex requirements is essential to determining its practical reliability. Conducting such an evaluation demands agentic tasks that reconcile broad domain coverage with deep procedural complexity. Existing paradigms face a fundamental trade-off: expert-curated benchmarks achieve procedural depth but suffer from domain diversity bottlenecks, whereas crowdsourced benchmarks span diverse scenarios but lack execution rigor. To resolve this tension, we present SkillForgeBench, which leverages agent skills as unifying execution blueprints across three co-designed stages: (1) Resource Preparation uses domain-balanced skills to guide artifact retrieval, establishing broad workflow coverage; (2) Skill-driven Task Synthesis employs skills as procedural anchors to partition artifact roles, synthesize naturalistic task instructions, and package sandboxed scenarios for deep procedural execution; and (3) Holistic Evaluation Design translates skill-embedded requirements into fine-grained completion and process rubrics to enable strict requirement-level scoring. Comprising 842 sandboxed tasks across five major professional domains, SkillForgeBench reveals a steep capability hierarchy among eight mainstream agents, led by Opus 4.8 ($40.9\%$ strict completion). Requiring all constrains satisfaction uncovers severe capability bottlenecks across all model tiers that remain masked under individual rubric pass rates ($70.0\%$ to $85.7\%$ vs. $12.6\%$ to $40.9\%$). Crucially, under the external guidance from skills, top-tier agents obtain larger gains than low-tier agents (i.e., $\Delta 19.2\%$ for Opus 4.8 vs. $\Delta 5.8\%$ for Haiku 4.5). Finally, agents satisfy process rubrics far less frequently than completion rubrics (under $3\%$ for Haiku 4.5), proving that completion-only checks overestimate real-world agent reliability.

replace-cross SkillScreen: High-Recall Screening of Malicious Agent Skills with Decision Models

Authors: Jerrold Huang, Junwei Quan, Saiyue Lyu, Zihan Guo, Yingqiang Ge

Abstract: Agent skills turn natural-language instructions into actions, creating a security challenge: legitimate automation and malicious behaviour can look deceptively similar. A practical screen must catch nearly every attack while keeping the review load manageable. We present SkillScreen, a compact decision model trained to score skill maliciousness directly from SKILL$.$md in a single forward pass. On MaliciousSkillBench, SkillScreen flags 20-25% of benign skills at 99% recall on unseen attack families, substantially fewer than the word-level classifier and prompted LLM judges tested. Under the same training protocol, it also generalizes better than a fully fine-tuned encoder to unseen sources. Yet unfamiliar benign sources sharply increase false positives, showing that recognizing new attacks does not ensure reliable screening of new skill collections. Our results demonstrate the value of task-trained decision models for high-recall skill screening. Project page: https://jerroldh.github.io/SkillScreen-webpage/

URLs: https://jerroldh.github.io/SkillScreen-webpage/

replace-cross Match the Canvas, Not Just the Answer: Predictability-Aware SFT for Diffusion Language Models

Authors: Miao Li, Hanyang Jiang, Hengyu Fu, Baihe Huang, Yating Liu, Yuhang Cai, Changkun Guan, Zhongzhu Chen, Jiecheng Lu, Michael A. Boateng, Pascal Van Hentenryck

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) enable flexible parallel generation by resolving tokens according to the model's evolving predictability rather than a fixed left-to-right order. This flexibility, however, introduces training choices absent from standard autoregressive teacher forcing: which reference tokens are visible when a target is supervised and how much generation space is allocated. Standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT) typically makes these choices through random masking, independently of inference. Our analysis shows how this training-inference mismatch can misdirect supervision. Random masking can conceal prediction errors or penalize uncertainty that decoding would otherwise resolve, while the attention-visible input length can change which response formulations the model favors, even when the underlying solution is unchanged. Motivated by these findings, we propose Predictability-Aware Canvas Selection and Supervision (PACS), an SFT method. PACS uses the model's own predictability to select each example's attention-visible input length and supervises reference tokens immediately before their confidence-guided revelation. Across reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, PACS consistently achieves the highest mean accuracy among the compared SFT methods, with gains of up to 12.84 percentage points over vanilla SFT on individual benchmarks. Code will be released upon publication.

replace-cross Which Model for Which Role? Cost-Efficient Pareto Recommendation for LLM Agentic Workflows

Authors: Qian Xie, Yueli He, Wenyue Hua, Sripad Karne, Armaan Agrawal, Nikos Pagonas, Eugene Wu, Kostis Kaffes, Tianyi Peng, Nairen Cao

Abstract: LLM agentic workflows increasingly employ different models for specialized roles. Users may prefer different trade-offs between task quality and deployment cost, sometimes also considering latency. Finding good assignments incurs a separate search cost from evaluating multiple candidates. We introduce Cost-Coupled Gittins (CC-Gittins), a multi-objective extension of single-objective cost-aware Gittins search for Pareto recommendation. It exploits the coupling between evaluation and deployment costs: assignments that cost more to deploy also cost more to evaluate. The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. We evaluate CC-Gittins on eight quality--cost matrices from workflows with two to five roles and up to 1,681 candidate assignments. We also evaluate the method with latency as a third objective. Across the eight benchmarks, CC-Gittins recovers a substantial fraction of the true Pareto frontier at roughly 10% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost, and recovers most of the frontier with few false positives by 30%.

replace-cross Typed Knowledge over Constructive Institutions: A Proof-Relevant Type Theory for Verified Science

Authors: Hans-Martin Will

Abstract: Proof-assistant kernels with strict propositions---Lean 4, Agda's Prop, and Coq/Rocq's SProp---build proof irrelevance into conversion ($p \equiv q$ for $p, q : P$), equating all derivations of a theorem. While natural for pure mathematics, judgmental proof irrelevance breaks provenance in verified scientific stores, where predicates, refinements, and institutional maps must distinguish independent derivations for consilience and counterfactual retraction; with subsingleton elimination, it also breaks normalization and algorithmic transitivity. We present the type-theoretic and institutional foundations of Eigenius, a kernel for provenance-preserving scientific stores. First, we formulate a dependent kernel with cumulative universes (Prop, Set, $\mathsf{Type}_k$ for $k \ge 1$), impredicative Prop, inductive families with index-forced subsingleton elimination, and intensional identity types: conversion $\equiv$ is untyped orthogonal-CRS joinability, while proof irrelevance is a witnessed axiom ($\mathsf{irrel} : \Pi P:\mathsf{Prop}.\,\Pi p,q:P.\,\mathsf{Id}\,P\,p\,q$). Second, coercion-free subsumption unifies cumulativity, $\Pi$-covariant subtyping, and ontology inclusions; its bidirectional fragments ($\mathcal{K}_0, \mathcal{K}_1$) satisfy subject reduction, match declarative typing, and compile to heuristic-free checkers ($\mathcal{A}_0, \mathcal{A}_1$) sound and complete relative to strong normalization. Third, we link external verifiers via constructive institutions (Adopt, Discharge, Relativize), internalize justification logic with Why-provenance $\mathsf{supp}(j,P)$, and type open-world knowledge graphs via TTR. Finally, we prove consistency in Grothendieck universes and mechanize the metatheory in Lean 4 with zero sorry.

replace-cross PACE: More Agents, More Samples, or More Thinking? Untangling Coordination, Ensembling, and Test-Time Compute in Multi-Agent LLMs

Authors: Seyed Mahmoud Sajjadi Mohammadabadi

Abstract: Multi-agent language model evaluations often compare a communicating system with a single greedy pass, which confounds coordination with extra samples and test-time compute. We introduce Parity-Aware Compute Evaluation (PACE): a six-dimensional resource vector, four baseline categories, and a directional rule that credits a gain only against a baseline using at least as much of the declared resources and rules a gain out against any baseline using no more. We audit 9 published studies. Across the 72 deduplicated baseline cells that report both a greedy and a sampled baseline, the sampling gap, by which the sampled baseline lowers the multi-agent difference, averages -0.036 with studies weighted equally (negative in 64 cells), and 53 of the 123 paired contrasts that beat a greedy pass become nonpositive against the sampled baseline. Against sampled or budget-matched baselines the mean difference is -0.042; all eight Category B/C study means are negative and the one Category D study is positive. In a controlled experiment with an open-weight model, debate and mixture-of-agents do not clear the equal-token self-consistency frontier on MMLU-Pro (debate -0.015); on Hidden Profile, split-view debate exceeds a lower-budget pool yet trails content-mismatched placebo and four-choice chance. We release a seven-item checklist, an evaluation card, and command-line utilities.

replace-cross Seen but Unsaid: Locating and Repairing Cross-Image Comparison in Vision-Language Models

Authors: Zhaoning Zhang

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) often fail to match a point or an object across two images, even when the match can be decoded from their hidden states. We trace where this information is lost in Qwen3-VL-2B/4B/8B/32B, InternVL3.5-8B, and Gemma-3-12B on synthetic shape matching, HPatches point correspondence, and SPair-71k semantic correspondence, comparing input-matched probes with calibrated outputs. The models fail at two distinct points. Exact copies are compared and the result reaches the answer position, but the output does not use it, whether or not the shapes have names. Semantic correspondences are present in the visual tokens but are only weakly compared and never reach the answer position. The comparison runs through 16 to 32 attention heads that let tokens of the second image attend to the first. Redistributing their attention at fixed mass removes up to 32 points of accuracy, against at most 7 for random or attention-matched heads, and their attention is gated by salience and weighted by content. Training only these heads (0.02% of the parameters) recovers 25 to 28 points on SPair-71k and transfers to HPatches and BLINK. Unconstrained LoRA adapters, which reach higher accuracy, converge on the same heads: inserting only these heads' attention into the base model recovers 21-51% of an adapter's gain, mostly through changed inputs to the heads. Across all 14 trained arms, the selectivity of this small head set tracks the accuracy they reach, and it is read directly from attention, without probes or retraining. The gain in selectivity is not a by-product of more attention to the first image: it comes mostly from the wrong options no longer being drawn to the marked reference.

replace-cross SkillBuffBench: Measuring What Agent Skills Contribute across Harnesses, Models, Skill Access Modes and Memory

Authors: Gengyue Dong, Ye Wei, Zheng Zhu, Yidong Han, Jiale Miao, Guanghui Li

Abstract: Agent Skills, file-based packages of procedural knowledge that a coding agent can discover and load on demand, are now shipped by every major agent harness, yet the evidence for how much they help comes almost entirely from settings in which the right Skill is hand-picked for the task and the agent starts from a blank slate. Neither holds in deployment, where an agent chooses from whatever library happens to be installed and frequently carries memory from earlier work. We present SkillBuffBench, a benchmark and paired evaluation protocol that treats Skill access as a three-level factor (no Skill, a curated Skill, and automatic invocation from an installed library), crosses it with memory (off and on), with four agent harnesses and with multiple models (the state-of-the-art proprietary models from Anthropic, Google and OpenAI, and the state-of-the-art open-weight models from Chinese providers), every model being run under every harness, and evaluates the result on research tasks contributed by domain experts together with the Skills they use, currently in materials science and biology, with the suite extended on a rolling basis. Every run is marked by a deterministic verifier; uncertainty is reported with a paired, task-level bootstrap; and cost, agent rounds and wall-clock time are reported alongside the score rather than folded into it. This version of the paper describes the design of the benchmark, its evaluation protocol and its current tasks and Skills. The evaluation is in progress: results will be added once the runs are complete, and the task suite, results and analysis will continue to be updated on a rolling basis.

replace-cross Thinking Harder Doesn't Lead to Better Choices: Reasoning Effort Increases the Confidence but Not the Correctness of LLM Shopping Agents

Authors: Adi Krishnamoorthy, X. Y. Han

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous shopping agents that process multimodal product listings (consisting of visual and text data) and recommend purchases on behalf of consumers. Naturally, one might assume that models with more thinking power would make higher-quality purchasing decisions. To test this belief, we ask two questions about such agents: does asking a model to think harder make it choose better products, and does the confidence it reports tell us the likelihood it may be wrong? We benchmark ten frontier models on three product-choice benchmarks built from WebShop, Amazon's SQID and Wayfair's WANDS platforms to see if LLMs with more thinking power actually make better-quality product purchase decisions. Across all three benchmarks, we found that increasing the thinking effort does not increase decision quality in a statistically meaningful way. The same change in thinking effort raises stated confidence at a statistically significant level, so extra thinking buys confidence rather than correctness. We also measure the calibration of these models (how well their reported confidence percentage matches their true correctness percentage) in these tasks and show that most models are not well-calibrated at every effort level. We also find that errors are strongly shared: when one model errs in a task, several others tend to err on that same task. Furthermore, on tasks where several models all err, a large number of them pick the same wrong product.

replace-cross Steering Automated Scientific Discovery via Audited Epistemic Exploration

Authors: Fangxu Yu, Tao Feng, Xuying Ning, Haolin Yang, Jiaxuan You, Tianyi Zhou

Abstract: Scientific discovery requires revisiting the hypotheses in light of new evidence and identifying promising directions beyond those already explored. However, research agents often make claims that are not supported by their observations, explore a narrow hypothesis space, and retain beliefs despite recognizing conflicting evidence. These failures motivate the search for an explicit epistemic state that captures both beliefs about existing hypotheses and directions for further exploration. To this end, we propose Epistemic Compass (EpiCompass), a plug-and-play scaffold that maintains this state through an epistemic tree linking hypotheses, evidence, beliefs, and revisions. As an investigation progresses, EpiCompass reassesses the hypotheses explored against accumulated evidence and uses unresolved conflicts and unexplored directions to guide hypothesis generation. It selects candidates by anticipated epistemic gain, accounting for both refinement of existing hypotheses and expansion of the hypothesis space, and verifies the final conclusion against the accumulated evidence. Experimental results across three scientific discovery benchmarks and three LLM backbones demonstrate that EpiCompass consistently outperforms baselines, with further analyses showing reductions in errors of unsupported claims and failures to revise beliefs. Our code is available at https://github.com/tianyi-lab/EpiCompass.

URLs: https://github.com/tianyi-lab/EpiCompass.

replace-cross GLiDE: A Fast, Calibrated Decision Model Distilled from Reasoning Teachers

Authors: Sahibzada Allahyar, Julia White, Dhruv Atreja, George Hurn-Maloney, Urchade Zaratiana

Abstract: Decision models score candidate answers in a single forward pass, but the intermediate reasoning that improves decision quality adds latency. We introduce GLiDE, a family of decision models from 0.4B to 27B parameters that distills reasoning into single-pass inference. Building on Qwen's hybrid architecture, GLiDE removes causal masking from full-attention layers so that context, question and candidate answers interact bidirectionally. It is trained with soft-target distillation from multiple teachers, including a reasoning model, and progressive post-training on diverse decision tasks. On the Hugging Face Decision Index, each of the five GLiDE models outscores every alternative of equal or smaller size. GLiDE V1.2 (27B) ranks first on edition 0.4.1 (22 October 2026) with a hidden-set score of 71.52, 2.0% calibration error and 92.9 ms median latency on one GPU, and shares first place on all three versions of the Artificial Analysis Decision Index. Both leaderboards are run by their maintainers on closed test sets. With checkpoint averaging and recurrent depth, GLiDE 27B reaches 73.37.

replace-cross PaMuk-Bench: A Parallel Multilingual Multitask Benchmark for Vision-Language Tasks

Authors: Silviu-Florin Gheorghe, Radu-Stefan Popa, Adriana Valentina Costache, Adrian-Gabriel Chifu, Alessia Amelio, Paul Irofti, Anders Skaarup Johansen, Lallouani Bouchakour, Mohamed Lichouri, Khaled Lounnas, Neelu Madan, Yashvir Malik, Radu Tudor Ionescu

Abstract: While vision-language models (VLMs) currently obtain notable results for highly-studied languages, e.g. English, they exhibit significant performance drops in certain low-resource languages, especially due to the lack of annotated datasets. To this end, we propose PaMuk-Bench, a novel parallel multilingual multitask benchmark for vision-language tasks. Our benchmark includes a set of over 10K curated English prompts that are translated into six languages: Arabic, Danish, French, Hindi, Italian, Romanian. Parallel translations, which are obtained via a semi-automatic process, along with accompanying generated and retrieved images enable the comparative development and evaluation of VLMs across a wide range of tasks: text-to-image generation, image captioning, content-based image retrieval, image generation difficulty estimation, image retrieval difficulty estimation. We empirically show that zero-shot English and multilingual pre-trained VLMs compare consistently worse than their fine-tuned counterparts, confirming that PaMuk-Bench is a valuable resource that can drastically reduce the language gap across a variety of VLM architectures and tasks. PaMuk-Bench is available at https://github.com/ghesil/pamuk_bench.

URLs: https://github.com/ghesil/pamuk_bench.
